========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess this whole change in me... it’s been going on for about two weeks now. It started right around the time my daughter left Georgia for the military. She actually drove down there herself. I told her to make sure she checked the oil and the tire pressure before she hit the highway, you know? Kids these days, they just get in and drive. They don't think about the belts or the hoses or anything. I remember back in '92, I had this old Ford truck, and the radiator hose blew right in the middle of a bridge during rush hour. Steam going everywhere, people honking. I had to walk three miles to a gas station in the pouring rain just to get a jug of coolant. Ruined my favorite pair of work boots, too. Had to throw 'em right in the trash. But anyway... yeah, since she packed up and left for the army two weeks ago, that's when I really started having those thoughts about them not needing me around.

Well, no, not really. I used to love messing with my tomato plants, you know? I had this whole system going with the stakes and the netting. motorcycle parts on credit like it was no big deal, and only later did I look at the bills and wonder what in the world I was thinking.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I guess I'm here because I told my supervisor something, and he thought I should come in. Nowadays it's all that cheap pine, warps if you just look at it wrong. I remember going to three different lumber yards trying to find the right pieces for that chest. The guy at the last place, Jimmy, he had a lazy eye, but he knew his wood. Anyway, I used to do all that, and watch the Falcons on Sunday, and stay after church talking with folks. But lately... I don't know, for the past couple weeks, I just look at all that stuff and it feels like it belongs to somebody else. No, not a thing. Like I was saying, I used to get so much out of messing with my tomato plants. I had the Big Boys and the Early Girls, and I'd tie them up with those little green Velcro strips. I tried telling my wife that once when she bought the cheap ones, but she didn't want to hear it. Anyway, yeah... no pleasure in anything. Just feeling empty.

Slowed down? Yeah, that's exactly it. It feels like my whole body is just moving through thick mud. Even the guys at work are noticing. They keep coming up to me, asking if I'm coming down with a bug or if I'm sick. I manage the floor over at the parts warehouse, and usually I'm walking laps around that place, keeping everyone on their toes. We got this one kid on the forklift, Tommy. He's barely twenty, drives that thing like it's a go-kart. I told him a hundred times he's gonna take down a whole pallet rack if he takes the corners that fast. Back in the day, if you drove a lift like that, the foreman would have your badge before lunch. They didn't mess around with safety back in the 80s, I'll tell you that. You wore your hard hat or you went home without pay. But anyway... yeah, Tommy and the other guys, they keep asking if I'm okay because I'm just dragging my feet. I'm walking so much slower than usual. And when they ask me simple questions, like about the shipping logs or where to put a crate, it just takes me forever to get the answer out. My brain just can't find the words fast enough. It's just... mud. Everything is mud.

I made this beautiful cedar chest for my wife back in, oh, must have been '98. The wood grain on that thing was just incredible. You don't see cedar like that anymore at the hardware store. I don't care about it at all. It's funny, speaking of church, the pastor's wife, Mrs. Higgins, she always brings this casserole to the potluck. It's got tater tots on top. Just feeling empty.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or slowed down in your movements over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Slowed down? Yeah, that's exactly it. It feels like my whole body is just moving through thick mud. Even the guys at work are noticing. They keep coming up to me, asking if I'm coming down with a bug or if I'm sick. I manage the floor over at the parts warehouse, and usually I'm walking laps around that place, keeping everyone on their toes. We got this one kid on the forklift, Tommy. He's barely twenty, drives that thing like it's a go-kart. I told him a hundred times he's gonna take down a whole pallet rack if he takes the corners that fast. Back in the day, if you drove a lift like that, the foreman would have your badge before lunch. They didn't mess around with safety back in the 80s, I'll tell you that. You wore your hard hat or you went home without pay. But anyway... yeah, Tommy and the other guys, they keep asking if I'm okay because I'm just dragging my feet. I'm walking so much slower than usual. And when they ask me simple questions, like about the shipping logs or where to put a crate, it just takes me forever to get the answer out. My brain just can't find the words fast enough. It's just... mud. Well, no, I haven't really thought about *how* I'd do it. It's not like I'm sitting there making a plan with ropes or pills or anything like that. It's more just... this heavy feeling, you know? Like I said, I just keep having these thoughts that my wife and kids would be better off if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. That's actually why I'm here. It spooked me so bad I finally said something to my supervisor, Dave, down at the warehouse. Dave's a good guy. He's been there almost as long as I have. We started back when the company was still owned by old man Miller. Miller used to come down to the floor every Christmas and hand out turkeys to everyone. Real turkeys, not those frozen butterball things they give you coupons for nowadays. He'd shake your hand and ask about your family. You don't see bosses doing that anymore. Now it's all corporate guys in suits who wouldn't know a pallet jack from a hole in the ground. They came in last year and tried to change the whole inventory system. A disaster, complete disaster. But anyway, yeah, I told Dave about the thoughts, and he's the one who said I needed to come get checked out. So no, no plan to actually hurt myself, just wishing I wouldn't wake up.

He's barely twenty, drives that thing like it's a go-kart. I told him a hundred times he's gonna take down a whole pallet rack if he takes the corners that fast. It spooked me so bad I finally said something to my supervisor, Dave, down at the warehouse. Dave's a good guy. He's been there almost as long as I have. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty? Yeah... I mean, mostly I just feel like I'm no good to them. I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it in my head, and telling myself I must have failed her somehow. Like a better father would have done something different, you know? Said the right thing, or been there more. But she just packed up her little Honda Civic and drove out to Oregon. Portland, I think it was. 

It rains a lot out there. I made sure to check her tires before she hit the road. You gotta have good tread if you're driving out to the Pacific Northwest. I put a set of Michelin Defenders on there for her. You can't cheap out on tires. People buy those budget brands at the big box stores to save fifty bucks, and next thing they know they're hydroplaning into a guardrail. My buddy Sal, he runs a tire shop off Route 9, and he says he sees it all the time. Sal's a good guy. He's got this old basset hound that just sleeps right under the hydraulic lift all day. Doesn't even flinch when the air tools kick on. Just snores right through it. 

But anyway... yeah. I just sit around thinking I failed her, and that they'd all just be doing better if I wasn't in the picture.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Worthless? I guess that's the word for it. It all just comes back to my daughter, really. I just keep replaying her leaving in my head over and over. I just sit there telling myself I must have failed her somehow, you know? Like if I was a better father, I would have done something different, said something different to make her stay. Or maybe just been there more when she was younger. 

Kids grow up so fast. One minute you're teaching them how to ride a bike without training wheels, and the next they're packing up their car to move across the country. I taught both my kids to ride on this old Schwinn I picked up at a garage sale. Best ten bucks I ever spent. They don't make bikes like that anymore, all solid steel frame. Heavy as a tank, but you could drop it off a roof and it wouldn't dent. Nowadays everything is aluminum or carbon fiber. People spend thousands of dollars on these fancy road bikes just to ride around the park on a Sunday. I see them sometimes when I drive past Memorial Park, all decked out in the tight spandex shorts like they're in the Tour de France. It's ridiculous. My neighbor, Gary, he bought one of those things. Must have cost him two grand. He rode it twice and now it just hangs in his garage next to his golf clubs. Gary's always buying stuff like that. He got a riding mower last year for a lawn that's barely bigger than a postage stamp. 

But yeah... I just feel like I wasn't the dad I should have been, and now she's gone, and I can't fix it. It just makes me feel like I'm no good to any of them.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Weight? Yeah, I suppose I have. I don't know exactly how much, I don't really step on the scale much anymore. But my belts are definitely looser. Most days I have to force myself to just eat a few bites. Even the stuff I usually like... like, my wife makes this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Has for thirty years. She puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, gives it this nice crust. But lately, she puts it in front of me and it just sits there until I end up throwing it out. 

Same thing at work. I used to look forward to the lunch truck pulling up at the warehouse. The guy who runs it, Hector, he makes these amazing Cuban sandwiches. Real roasted pork, not the deli sliced stuff. Hector's a great guy. He bought that truck off his cousin about five years ago and completely gutted it, put all new stainless steel in. He was telling me the other day about how much a commercial flat top grill costs now. It's highway robbery, what they charge for restaurant equipment. You'd think it was plated in gold. And the permits? Don't even get me started on what the city makes him pay just to park the thing. 

But yeah, even Hector's sandwiches... I just don't have the stomach for them right now. I just can't seem to make myself eat.

My buddy Sal, he runs a tire shop off Route 9, and he says he sees it all the time. Sal's a good guy. He's got this old basset hound that just sleeps right under the hydraulic lift all day. Doesn't even flinch when the air tools kick on. Just snores right through it. \n\nBut anyway... yeah. I just sit around thinking I failed her, and that they'd all just be doing better if I wasn't in the picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? I guess that's the word for it. It all just comes back to my daughter, really. I just keep replaying her leaving in my head over and over. I just sit there telling myself I must have failed her somehow, you know? Like if I was a better father, I would have done something different, said something different to make her stay. Or maybe just been there more when she was younger. \n\nKids grow up so fast. One minute you're teaching them how to ride a bike without training wheels, and the next they're packing up their car to move across the country. I taught both my kids to ride on this old Schwinn I picked up at a garage sale. Best ten bucks I ever spent. They don't make bikes like that anymore, all solid steel frame. Heavy as a tank, but you could drop it off a roof and it wouldn't dent. Nowadays everything is aluminum or carbon fiber. People spend thousands of dollars on these fancy road bikes just to ride around the park on a Sunday. I see them sometimes when I drive past Memorial Park, all decked out in the tight spandex shorts like they're in the Tour de France. It's ridiculous. My neighbor, Gary, he bought one of those things. Must have cost him two grand. He rode it twice and now it just hangs in his garage next to his golf clubs. Gary's always buying stuff like that. He got a riding mower last year for a lawn that's barely bigger than a postage stamp. \n\nBut yeah... I just feel like I wasn't the dad I should have been, and now she's gone, and I can't fix it. It just makes me feel like I'm no good to any of them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I suppose I have. I don't know exactly how much, I don't really step on the scale much anymore. But my belts are definitely looser. Most days I have to force myself to just eat a few bites. Even the stuff I usually like... like, my wife makes this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Has for thirty years. She puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, gives it this nice crust. But lately, she puts it in front of me and it just sits there until I end up throwing it out. \n\nSame thing at work. I used to look forward to the lunch truck pulling up at the warehouse. The guy who runs it, Hector, he makes these amazing Cuban sandwiches. Real roasted pork, not the deli sliced stuff. Hector's a great guy. He bought that truck off his cousin about five years ago and completely gutted it, put all new stainless steel in. He was telling me the other day about how much a commercial flat top grill costs now. It's highway robbery, what they charge for restaurant equipment. You'd think it was plated in gold. And the permits? Don't even get me started on what the city makes him pay just to park the thing. \n\nBut yeah, even Hector's sandwiches... I just don't have the stomach for them right now. I just can't seem to make myself eat.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling restless or fidgety over the past two weeks?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Restless? No, not really. It's actually the other way around. I feel like my whole body is just... moving through mud. The guys at work keep asking me if I'm sick or something, because they see I'm walking a lot slower, and I'm taking forever just to answer simple questions. 

I manage the operations over at the distribution center, and usually I'm all over the floor. We've got a hundred and fifty thousand square feet of rack space. We use these Raymond reach trucks to pull the pallets down from the top racks. Great machines, really. They run on these massive industrial batteries that weigh about two thousand pounds each. You gotta use a special hoist just to swap them out at the charging station. One time, this kid on the night shift—good kid, but a little careless—he didn't hook the spreader bar up right and almost dropped one of those batteries right onto the concrete floor. The acid in those things is no joke. It'll eat right through your steel-toe boots like they're paper. You gotta wear the thick rubber apron and the face shield when you water them, OSHA requires it. The safety inspector we get, this guy Stan, he's a real stickler for that stuff. He'll write you up just for having your safety glasses resting up on your hard hat instead of over your eyes. Stan doesn't mess around. 

But anyway... no, I'm not fidgety. Just... slow. It just takes me a long time to process what anybody is saying to me lately.

I used to love messing with my tomato plants, you know? I had this whole system going with the stakes and the netting. And I'd spend hours sanding wood at my workbench out in the garage. Nowadays it's all that cheap pine, warps if you just look at it wrong. I remember going to three different lumber yards trying to find the right pieces for that chest. I just don't have the care for it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure in doing anything at all over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not a thing. Just feeling empty.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or slowed down in your movements over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Slowed down? Yeah, that's exactly it. I manage the floor over at the parts warehouse, and usually I'm walking laps around that place, keeping everyone on their toes. We got this one kid on the forklift, Tommy. He's barely twenty, drives that thing like it's a go-kart. I told him a hundred times he's gonna take down a whole pallet rack if he takes the corners that fast. It spooked me so bad I finally said something to my supervisor, Dave, down at the warehouse. Dave's a good guy. He's been there almost as long as I have. We started back when the company was still owned by old man Miller. Miller used to come down to the floor every Christmas and hand out turkeys to everyone. Real turkeys, not those frozen butterball things they give you coupons for nowadays. He'd shake your hand and ask about your family. You don't see bosses doing that anymore. Now it's all corporate guys in suits who wouldn't know a pallet jack from a hole in the ground. They came in last year and tried to change the whole inventory system. A disaster, complete disaster. I think she uses the name-brand ones, Ore-Ida, because they get really crispy. The store-brand ones just turn to mush, you know? I tried telling my wife that once when she bought the cheap ones, but she didn't want to hear it. Anyway, yeah... no pleasure in anything. Just feeling empty.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or slowed down in your movements over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Slowed down? Yeah, that's exactly it. It feels like my whole body is just moving through thick mud. Even the guys at work are noticing. They keep coming up to me, asking if I'm coming down with a bug or if I'm sick. I manage the floor over at the parts warehouse, and usually I'm walking laps around that place, keeping everyone on their toes. We got this one kid on the forklift, Tommy. He's barely twenty, drives that thing like it's a go-kart. I told him a hundred times he's gonna take down a whole pallet rack if he takes the corners that fast. Back in the day, if you drove a lift like that, the foreman would have your badge before lunch. They didn't mess around with safety back in the 80s, I'll tell you that. You wore your hard hat or you went home without pay. But anyway... yeah, Tommy and the other guys, they keep asking if I'm okay because I'm just dragging my feet. I'm walking so much slower than usual. And when they ask me simple questions, like about the shipping logs or where to put a crate, it just takes me forever to get the answer out. My brain just can't find the words fast enough. It's just... mud. Everything is mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would harm yourself if you were to act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't really thought about *how* I'd do it. It's not like I'm sitting there making a plan with ropes or pills or anything like that. It's more just... this heavy feeling, you know? Like I said, I just keep having these thoughts that my wife and kids would be better off if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. That's actually why I'm here. It spooked me so bad I finally said something to my supervisor, Dave, down at the warehouse. Dave's a good guy. He's been there almost as long as I have. We started back when the company was still owned by old man Miller. Miller used to come down to the floor every Christmas and hand out turkeys to everyone. Real turkeys, not those frozen butterball things they give you coupons for nowadays. He'd shake your hand and ask about your family. You don't see bosses doing that anymore. Now it's all corporate guys in suits who wouldn't know a pallet jack from a hole in the ground. They came in last year and tried to change the whole inventory system. A disaster, complete disaster. But anyway, yeah, I told Dave about the thoughts, and he's the one who said I needed to come get checked out. So no, no plan to actually hurt myself, just wishing I wouldn't wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about having these thoughts that your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... I mean, mostly I just feel like I'm no good to them. I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it in my head, and telling myself I must have failed her somehow. Like a better father would have done something different, you know? Said the right thing, or been there more. But she just packed up her little Honda Civic and drove out to Oregon. Portland, I think it was. \n\nIt rains a lot out there. I made sure to check her tires before she hit the road. You gotta have good tread if you're driving out to the Pacific Northwest. I put a set of Michelin Defenders on there for her. You can't cheap out on tires. People buy those budget brands at the big box stores to save fifty bucks, and next thing they know they're hydroplaning into a guardrail. My buddy Sal, he runs a tire shop off Route 9, and he says he sees it all the time. Sal's a good guy. He's got this old basset hound that just sleeps right under the hydraulic lift all day. Doesn't even flinch when the air tools kick on. Just snores right through it. \n\nBut anyway... yeah. I just sit around thinking I failed her, and that they'd all just be doing better if I wasn't in the picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? I guess that's the word for it. It all just comes back to my daughter, really. I just keep replaying her leaving in my head over and over. I just sit there telling myself I must have failed her somehow, you know? Like if I was a better father, I would have done something different, said something different to make her stay. Or maybe just been there more when she was younger. \n\nKids grow up so fast. One minute you're teaching them how to ride a bike without training wheels, and the next they're packing up their car to move across the country. I taught both my kids to ride on this old Schwinn I picked up at a garage sale. Best ten bucks I ever spent. They don't make bikes like that anymore, all solid steel frame. Heavy as a tank, but you could drop it off a roof and it wouldn't dent. Nowadays everything is aluminum or carbon fiber. People spend thousands of dollars on these fancy road bikes just to ride around the park on a Sunday. I see them sometimes when I drive past Memorial Park, all decked out in the tight spandex shorts like they're in the Tour de France. It's ridiculous. My neighbor, Gary, he bought one of those things. Must have cost him two grand. He rode it twice and now it just hangs in his garage next to his golf clubs. Gary's always buying stuff like that. He got a riding mower last year for a lawn that's barely bigger than a postage stamp. \n\nBut yeah... I just feel like I wasn't the dad I should have been, and now she's gone, and I can't fix it. It just makes me feel like I'm no good to any of them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I suppose I have. I don't know exactly how much, I don't really step on the scale much anymore. But my belts are definitely looser. Most days I have to force myself to just eat a few bites. Even the stuff I usually like... like, my wife makes this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Has for thirty years. She puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, gives it this nice crust. But lately, she puts it in front of me and it just sits there until I end up throwing it out. \n\nSame thing at work. I used to look forward to the lunch truck pulling up at the warehouse. The guy who runs it, Hector, he makes these amazing Cuban sandwiches. Real roasted pork, not the deli sliced stuff. Hector's a great guy. He bought that truck off his cousin about five years ago and completely gutted it, put all new stainless steel in. He was telling me the other day about how much a commercial flat top grill costs now. It's highway robbery, what they charge for restaurant equipment. You'd think it was plated in gold. And the permits? Don't even get me started on what the city makes him pay just to park the thing. \n\nBut yeah, even Hector's sandwiches... I just don't have the stomach for them right now. I just can't seem to make myself eat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. It's actually the other way around. I feel like my whole body is just... moving through mud. The guys at work keep asking me if I'm sick or something, because they see I'm walking a lot slower, and I'm taking forever just to answer simple questions. \n\nI manage the operations over at the distribution center, and usually I'm all over the floor. We've got a hundred and fifty thousand square feet of rack space. We use these Raymond reach trucks to pull the pallets down from the top racks. Great machines, really. They run on these massive industrial batteries that weigh about two thousand pounds each. You gotta use a special hoist just to swap them out at the charging station. One time, this kid on the night shift—good kid, but a little careless—he didn't hook the spreader bar up right and almost dropped one of those batteries right onto the concrete floor. The acid in those things is no joke. It'll eat right through your steel-toe boots like they're paper. You gotta wear the thick rubber apron and the face shield when you water them, OSHA requires it. The safety inspector we get, this guy Stan, he's a real stickler for that stuff. He'll write you up just for having your safety glasses resting up on your hard hat instead of over your eyes. Stan doesn't mess around. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not fidgety. Just... slow. It just takes me a long time to process what anybody is saying to me lately.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, doc, like I was just saying... I really don't have any trouble sitting still. It's the exact opposite. Sitting is about all I want to do right now. Like I mentioned, I just feel heavy, like I'm moving through mud. That's why the guys at work are constantly asking me if I'm coming down with a bug. I'm just dragging my feet everywhere, and if they ask me something, it takes me forever just to get the words out to answer them. 

Tommy, our forklift lead, he was the one asking me yesterday if I had the flu. Which is pretty rich coming from him, because last winter he came in sick and took down half the morning shift. We were short-handed for a month. I had to call up this temp agency over on 4th street just to get warm bodies in the door to load the trucks. But man, the temps they send you these days... they don't have an ounce of common sense. This one kid showed up to his first shift wearing canvas sneakers. Canvas! Around five-ton machines! I told him, son, if a pallet jack clips your foot in those, they're gonna have to amputate your toes. I had to send him right back to the agency. You can't mess around with footwear in a warehouse. I've been wearing Red Wings since I was nineteen. They cost a pretty penny, but they last forever if you oil the leather right. You gotta use mink oil, not that synthetic stuff they sell at the big box stores. 

But yeah... no trouble sitting still. I'm just... really slow right now.

Just... thinking that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn't wake up in the morning. It kind of scared me to say it out loud, and it definitely spooked him, so... here I am.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess this whole change in me... it’s been going on for about two weeks now. It started right around the time my daughter left Georgia for the military. She actually drove down there herself. I told her to make sure she checked the oil and the tire pressure before she hit the highway, you know? Kids these days, they just get in and drive. They don't think about the belts or the hoses or anything. I remember back in '92, I had this old Ford truck, and the radiator hose blew right in the middle of a bridge during rush hour. Steam going everywhere, people honking. I had to walk three miles to a gas station in the pouring rain just to get a jug of coolant. Ruined my favorite pair of work boots, too. Had to throw 'em right in the trash. But anyway... yeah, since she packed up and left for the army two weeks ago, that's when I really started having those thoughts about them not needing me around.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I used to love messing with my tomato plants, you know? I had this whole system going with the stakes and the netting. And I'd spend hours sanding wood at my workbench out in the garage. I made this beautiful cedar chest for my wife back in, oh, must have been '98. The wood grain on that thing was just incredible. You don't see cedar like that anymore at the hardware store. Nowadays it's all that cheap pine, warps if you just look at it wrong. I remember going to three different lumber yards trying to find the right pieces for that chest. The guy at the last place, Jimmy, he had a lazy eye, but he knew his wood. Anyway, I used to do all that, and watch the Falcons on Sunday, and stay after church talking with folks. But lately... I don't know, for the past couple weeks, I just look at all that stuff and it feels like it belongs to somebody else. I just don't have the care for it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure in doing anything at all over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not a thing. Like I was saying, I used to get so much out of messing with my tomato plants. I had the Big Boys and the Early Girls, and I'd tie them up with those little green Velcro strips. And sanding wood at my bench out there... watching the Falcons, staying after church to talk with everybody... but these last two weeks, I just look at all of it and it's like... like it belongs to somebody else. I don't care about it at all. It's funny, speaking of church, the pastor's wife, Mrs. Higgins, she always brings this casserole to the potluck. It's got tater tots on top. I think she uses the name-brand ones, Ore-Ida, because they get really crispy. The store-brand ones just turn to mush, you know? I tried telling my wife that once when she bought the cheap ones, but she didn't want to hear it. Anyway, yeah... no pleasure in anything. Just feeling empty.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or slowed down in your movements over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Slowed down? Yeah, that's exactly it. It feels like my whole body is just moving through thick mud. Even the guys at work are noticing. They keep coming up to me, asking if I'm coming down with a bug or if I'm sick. I manage the floor over at the parts warehouse, and usually I'm walking laps around that place, keeping everyone on their toes. We got this one kid on the forklift, Tommy. He's barely twenty, drives that thing like it's a go-kart. I told him a hundred times he's gonna take down a whole pallet rack if he takes the corners that fast. Back in the day, if you drove a lift like that, the foreman would have your badge before lunch. They didn't mess around with safety back in the 80s, I'll tell you that. You wore your hard hat or you went home without pay. But anyway... yeah, Tommy and the other guys, they keep asking if I'm okay because I'm just dragging my feet. I'm walking so much slower than usual. And when they ask me simple questions, like about the shipping logs or where to put a crate, it just takes me forever to get the answer out. My brain just can't find the words fast enough. It's just... mud. Everything is mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would harm yourself if you were to act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't really thought about *how* I'd do it. It's not like I'm sitting there making a plan with ropes or pills or anything like that. It's more just... this heavy feeling, you know? Like I said, I just keep having these thoughts that my wife and kids would be better off if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. That's actually why I'm here. It spooked me so bad I finally said something to my supervisor, Dave, down at the warehouse. Dave's a good guy. He's been there almost as long as I have. We started back when the company was still owned by old man Miller. Miller used to come down to the floor every Christmas and hand out turkeys to everyone. Real turkeys, not those frozen butterball things they give you coupons for nowadays. He'd shake your hand and ask about your family. You don't see bosses doing that anymore. Now it's all corporate guys in suits who wouldn't know a pallet jack from a hole in the ground. They came in last year and tried to change the whole inventory system. A disaster, complete disaster. But anyway, yeah, I told Dave about the thoughts, and he's the one who said I needed to come get checked out. So no, no plan to actually hurt myself, just wishing I wouldn't wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about having these thoughts that your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... I mean, mostly I just feel like I'm no good to them. I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it in my head, and telling myself I must have failed her somehow. Like a better father would have done something different, you know? Said the right thing, or been there more. But she just packed up her little Honda Civic and drove out to Oregon. Portland, I think it was. \n\nIt rains a lot out there. I made sure to check her tires before she hit the road. You gotta have good tread if you're driving out to the Pacific Northwest. I put a set of Michelin Defenders on there for her. You can't cheap out on tires. People buy those budget brands at the big box stores to save fifty bucks, and next thing they know they're hydroplaning into a guardrail. My buddy Sal, he runs a tire shop off Route 9, and he says he sees it all the time. Sal's a good guy. He's got this old basset hound that just sleeps right under the hydraulic lift all day. Doesn't even flinch when the air tools kick on. Just snores right through it. \n\nBut anyway... yeah. I just sit around thinking I failed her, and that they'd all just be doing better if I wasn't in the picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? I guess that's the word for it. It all just comes back to my daughter, really. I just keep replaying her leaving in my head over and over. I just sit there telling myself I must have failed her somehow, you know? Like if I was a better father, I would have done something different, said something different to make her stay. Or maybe just been there more when she was younger. \n\nKids grow up so fast. One minute you're teaching them how to ride a bike without training wheels, and the next they're packing up their car to move across the country. I taught both my kids to ride on this old Schwinn I picked up at a garage sale. Best ten bucks I ever spent. They don't make bikes like that anymore, all solid steel frame. Heavy as a tank, but you could drop it off a roof and it wouldn't dent. Nowadays everything is aluminum or carbon fiber. People spend thousands of dollars on these fancy road bikes just to ride around the park on a Sunday. I see them sometimes when I drive past Memorial Park, all decked out in the tight spandex shorts like they're in the Tour de France. It's ridiculous. My neighbor, Gary, he bought one of those things. Must have cost him two grand. He rode it twice and now it just hangs in his garage next to his golf clubs. Gary's always buying stuff like that. He got a riding mower last year for a lawn that's barely bigger than a postage stamp. \n\nBut yeah... I just feel like I wasn't the dad I should have been, and now she's gone, and I can't fix it. It just makes me feel like I'm no good to any of them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I suppose I have. I don't know exactly how much, I don't really step on the scale much anymore. But my belts are definitely looser. Most days I have to force myself to just eat a few bites. Even the stuff I usually like... like, my wife makes this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Has for thirty years. She puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, gives it this nice crust. But lately, she puts it in front of me and it just sits there until I end up throwing it out. \n\nSame thing at work. I used to look forward to the lunch truck pulling up at the warehouse. The guy who runs it, Hector, he makes these amazing Cuban sandwiches. Real roasted pork, not the deli sliced stuff. Hector's a great guy. He bought that truck off his cousin about five years ago and completely gutted it, put all new stainless steel in. He was telling me the other day about how much a commercial flat top grill costs now. It's highway robbery, what they charge for restaurant equipment. You'd think it was plated in gold. And the permits? Don't even get me started on what the city makes him pay just to park the thing. \n\nBut yeah, even Hector's sandwiches... I just don't have the stomach for them right now. I just can't seem to make myself eat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. It's actually the other way around. I feel like my whole body is just... moving through mud. The guys at work keep asking me if I'm sick or something, because they see I'm walking a lot slower, and I'm taking forever just to answer simple questions. \n\nI manage the operations over at the distribution center, and usually I'm all over the floor. We've got a hundred and fifty thousand square feet of rack space. We use these Raymond reach trucks to pull the pallets down from the top racks. Great machines, really. They run on these massive industrial batteries that weigh about two thousand pounds each. You gotta use a special hoist just to swap them out at the charging station. One time, this kid on the night shift—good kid, but a little careless—he didn't hook the spreader bar up right and almost dropped one of those batteries right onto the concrete floor. The acid in those things is no joke. It'll eat right through your steel-toe boots like they're paper. You gotta wear the thick rubber apron and the face shield when you water them, OSHA requires it. The safety inspector we get, this guy Stan, he's a real stickler for that stuff. He'll write you up just for having your safety glasses resting up on your hard hat instead of over your eyes. Stan doesn't mess around. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not fidgety. Just... slow. It just takes me a long time to process what anybody is saying to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fidgety or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, doc, like I was just saying... I really don't have any trouble sitting still. It's the exact opposite. Sitting is about all I want to do right now. Like I mentioned, I just feel heavy, like I'm moving through mud. That's why the guys at work are constantly asking me if I'm coming down with a bug. I'm just dragging my feet everywhere, and if they ask me something, it takes me forever just to get the words out to answer them. \n\nTommy, our forklift lead, he was the one asking me yesterday if I had the flu. Which is pretty rich coming from him, because last winter he came in sick and took down half the morning shift. We were short-handed for a month. I had to call up this temp agency over on 4th street just to get warm bodies in the door to load the trucks. But man, the temps they send you these days... they don't have an ounce of common sense. This one kid showed up to his first shift wearing canvas sneakers. Canvas! Around five-ton machines! I told him, son, if a pallet jack clips your foot in those, they're gonna have to amputate your toes. I had to send him right back to the agency. You can't mess around with footwear in a warehouse. I've been wearing Red Wings since I was nineteen. They cost a pretty penny, but they last forever if you oil the leather right. You gotta use mink oil, not that synthetic stuff they sell at the big box stores. \n\nBut yeah... no trouble sitting still. I'm just... really slow right now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. No, I don't need to pace. I don't have any problem staying still. Like I keep saying, I'm just... I'm moving like molasses. My whole body feels like it's dragging through thick mud. That's why the guys at the warehouse keep asking if I'm sick. They see me walking slower, and taking forever just to answer a simple question. 

Speaking of questions, my wife asked me the other day if I thought we should get the gutters cleaned before the fall really hits. We've got these big oak trees in the front yard, beautiful trees, probably fifty, sixty years old. But man, the leaves they drop... it's a nightmare. Last year, the gutters backed up so bad the water was pouring right over the edge and washed out all her petunias. She was furious. We had this guy, Dave, who used to come out and do it for fifty bucks. He had this extendable ladder, real nice fiberglass one, not that aluminum junk that wobbles in the wind. But Dave moved down to Florida, somewhere near Tampa, I think. Said his arthritis couldn't take the winters up here anymore. I don't blame him. The cold gets into your joints as you get older. I feel it in my knees every time it rains. 

But yeah... no pacing. I'm just slow. Really, really slow.

We've got a hundred and fifty thousand square feet of rack space. We use these Raymond reach trucks to pull the pallets down from the top racks. Great machines, really. They run on these massive industrial batteries that weigh about two thousand pounds each. You gotta use a special hoist just to swap them out at the charging station. One time, this kid on the night shift—good kid, but a little careless—he didn't hook the spreader bar up right and almost dropped one of those batteries right onto the concrete floor. The acid in those things is no joke. It'll eat right through your steel-toe boots like they're paper. You gotta wear the thick rubber apron and the face shield when you water them, OSHA requires it. The safety inspector we get, this guy Stan, he's a real stickler for that stuff. He'll write you up just for having your safety glasses resting up on your hard hat instead of over your eyes. Stan doesn't mess around. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not fidgety. Just... slow. It just takes me a long time to process what anybody is saying to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fidgety or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, doc, like I was just saying... I really don't have any trouble sitting still. It's the exact opposite. Sitting is about all I want to do right now. Like I mentioned, I just feel heavy, like I'm moving through mud. That's why the guys at work are constantly asking me if I'm coming down with a bug. I'm just dragging my feet everywhere, and if they ask me something, it takes me forever just to get the words out to answer them. \n\nTommy, our forklift lead, he was the one asking me yesterday if I had the flu. Which is pretty rich coming from him, because last winter he came in sick and took down half the morning shift. We were short-handed for a month. I had to call up this temp agency over on 4th street just to get warm bodies in the door to load the trucks. But man, the temps they send you these days... they don't have an ounce of common sense. This one kid showed up to his first shift wearing canvas sneakers. Canvas! Around five-ton machines! I told him, son, if a pallet jack clips your foot in those, they're gonna have to amputate your toes. I had to send him right back to the agency. You can't mess around with footwear in a warehouse. I've been wearing Red Wings since I was nineteen. They cost a pretty penny, but they last forever if you oil the leather right. You gotta use mink oil, not that synthetic stuff they sell at the big box stores. \n\nBut yeah... no trouble sitting still. I'm just... really slow right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you need to pace back and forth or can't stay still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. No, I don't need to pace. I don't have any problem staying still. Like I keep saying, I'm just... I'm moving like molasses. My whole body feels like it's dragging through thick mud. That's why the guys at the warehouse keep asking if I'm sick. They see me walking slower, and taking forever just to answer a simple question. \n\nSpeaking of questions, my wife asked me the other day if I thought we should get the gutters cleaned before the fall really hits. We've got these big oak trees in the front yard, beautiful trees, probably fifty, sixty years old. But man, the leaves they drop... it's a nightmare. Last year, the gutters backed up so bad the water was pouring right over the edge and washed out all her petunias. She was furious. We had this guy, Dave, who used to come out and do it for fifty bucks. He had this extendable ladder, real nice fiberglass one, not that aluminum junk that wobbles in the wind. But Dave moved down to Florida, somewhere near Tampa, I think. Said his arthritis couldn't take the winters up here anymore. I don't blame him. The cold gets into your joints as you get older. I feel it in my knees every time it rains. \n\nBut yeah... no pacing. I'm just slow. Really, really slow.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your feelings of worthlessness and guilt are interfering with your ability to work or take care of things at home?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 57 / general_or_operations_manager / less_than_9th\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"At work, my mistakes got serious enough that a supervisor found me sitting in my truck before dawn and got occupational health involved. At home, I’ve let bills and responsibilities pile up instead of handling them like I normally would.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, they are. They really are. It's... it's getting bad at work. I've been making all these mistakes, just stupid things I'd never normally do. It got to the point where one of the upper supervisors actually found me sitting in my truck in the parking lot before dawn. I just... I couldn't bring myself to go inside. So he ended up getting occupational health involved, and now it's this whole big thing. 

Occupational health, they mean well, I guess. The nurse they have there, Brenda, she's been there since I started. She's got this little office right off the break room, always smells like rubbing alcohol and those stale vanilla air fresheners. Brenda's a good egg, though. She helped me out when I sliced my thumb open on a box cutter back in '98. Those box cutters, you gotta make sure you retract the blade all the way. We use the safety ones now, the ones where the blade snaps back on its own if you let go of the slider. Honestly, they're a pain to use when you're trying to break down heavy double-wall cardboard, but corporate mandated them after our workers' comp premiums went up. Insurance companies, they run everything now. You can't even change a lightbulb without filling out a risk assessment form. 

But yeah, at home too, it's just falling apart. I'm letting bills pile up on the counter. Stuff I'd usually handle right away, I just look at the envelopes and I can't even bring myself to open them. It's just too much.

My brain just can't find the words fast enough. It's just... mud. Like I said, I just keep having these thoughts that my wife and kids would be better off if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. That's actually why I'm here. It spooked me so bad I finally said something to my supervisor, Dave, down at the warehouse. Dave's a good guy. He's been there almost as long as I have. We started back when the company was still owned by old man Miller. Miller used to come down to the floor every Christmas and hand out turkeys to everyone. Real turkeys, not those frozen butterball things they give you coupons for nowadays. He'd shake your hand and ask about your family. You don't see bosses doing that anymore. Now it's all corporate guys in suits who wouldn't know a pallet jack from a hole in the ground. They came in last year and tried to change the whole inventory system. A disaster, complete disaster. But anyway, yeah, I told Dave about the thoughts, and he's the one who said I needed to come get checked out. So no, no plan to actually hurt myself, just wishing I wouldn't wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about having these thoughts that your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... I mean, mostly I just feel like I'm no good to them. I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it in my head, and telling myself I must have failed her somehow. Like a better father would have done something different, you know? Said the right thing, or been there more. But she just packed up her little Honda Civic and drove out to Oregon. Portland, I think it was. \n\nIt rains a lot out there. I made sure to check her tires before she hit the road. You gotta have good tread if you're driving out to the Pacific Northwest. I put a set of Michelin Defenders on there for her. You can't cheap out on tires. People buy those budget brands at the big box stores to save fifty bucks, and next thing they know they're hydroplaning into a guardrail. Doesn't even flinch when the air tools kick on. Just snores right through it. \n\nBut anyway... yeah. I just sit around thinking I failed her, and that they'd all just be doing better if I wasn't in the picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? I guess that's the word for it. It all just comes back to my daughter, really. I just keep replaying her leaving in my head over and over. I just sit there telling myself I must have failed her somehow, you know? Like if I was a better father, I would have done something different, said something different to make her stay. Or maybe just been there more when she was younger. \n\nKids grow up so fast. One minute you're teaching them how to ride a bike without training wheels, and the next they're packing up their car to move across the country. I taught both my kids to ride on this old Schwinn I picked up at a garage sale. Best ten bucks I ever spent. They don't make bikes like that anymore, all solid steel frame. Heavy as a tank, but you could drop it off a roof and it wouldn't dent. Nowadays everything is aluminum or carbon fiber. People spend thousands of dollars on these fancy road bikes just to ride around the park on a Sunday. Back in the day, if you drove a lift like that, the foreman would have your badge before lunch. They didn't mess around with safety back in the 80s, I'll tell you that. You wore your hard hat or you went home without pay. But anyway... yeah, Tommy and the other guys, they keep asking if I'm okay because I'm just dragging my feet. I'm walking so much slower than usual. And when they ask me simple questions, like about the shipping logs or where to put a crate, it just takes me forever to get the answer out. My brain just can't find the words fast enough. It's just... mud. Everything is mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would harm yourself if you were to act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't really thought about *how* I'd do it. It's not like I'm sitting there making a plan with ropes or pills or anything like that. It's more just... this heavy feeling, you know? Like I said, I just keep having these thoughts that my wife and kids would be better off if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. That's actually why I'm here. It spooked me so bad I finally said something to my supervisor, Dave, down at the warehouse. Dave's a good guy. He's been there almost as long as I have. We started back when the company was still owned by old man Miller. Miller used to come down to the floor every Christmas and hand out turkeys to everyone. Real turkeys, not those frozen butterball things they give you coupons for nowadays. He'd shake your hand and ask about your family. You don't see bosses doing that anymore. Now it's all corporate guys in suits who wouldn't know a pallet jack from a hole in the ground. They came in last year and tried to change the whole inventory system. A disaster, complete disaster. But anyway, yeah, I told Dave about the thoughts, and he's the one who said I needed to come get checked out. So no, no plan to actually hurt myself, just wishing I wouldn't wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about having these thoughts that your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... I mean, mostly I just feel like I'm no good to them. I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it in my head, and telling myself I must have failed her somehow. Like a better father would have done something different, you know? Said the right thing, or been there more. But she just packed up her little Honda Civic and drove out to Oregon. Portland, I think it was. \n\nIt rains a lot out there. I made sure to check her tires before she hit the road. You gotta have good tread if you're driving out to the Pacific Northwest. I put a set of Michelin Defenders on there for her. You can't cheap out on tires. People buy those budget brands at the big box stores to save fifty bucks, and next thing they know they're hydroplaning into a guardrail. My buddy Sal, he runs a tire shop off Route 9, and he says he sees it all the time. Sal's a good guy. He's got this old basset hound that just sleeps right under the hydraulic lift all day. Doesn't even flinch when the air tools kick on. Just snores right through it. \n\nBut anyway... yeah. I just sit around thinking I failed her, and that they'd all just be doing better if I wasn't in the picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? I guess that's the word for it. It all just comes back to my daughter, really. I just keep replaying her leaving in my head over and over. I just sit there telling myself I must have failed her somehow, you know? Like if I was a better father, I would have done something different, said something different to make her stay. Or maybe just been there more when she was younger. \n\nKids grow up so fast. One minute you're teaching them how to ride a bike without training wheels, and the next they're packing up their car to move across the country. I taught both my kids to ride on this old Schwinn I picked up at a garage sale. Best ten bucks I ever spent. They don't make bikes like that anymore, all solid steel frame. Heavy as a tank, but you could drop it off a roof and it wouldn't dent. Nowadays everything is aluminum or carbon fiber. People spend thousands of dollars on these fancy road bikes just to ride around the park on a Sunday. I see them sometimes when I drive past Memorial Park, all decked out in the tight spandex shorts like they're in the Tour de France. It's ridiculous. My neighbor, Gary, he bought one of those things. Must have cost him two grand. He rode it twice and now it just hangs in his garage next to his golf clubs. Gary's always buying stuff like that. He got a riding mower last year for a lawn that's barely bigger than a postage stamp. \n\nBut yeah... I just feel like I wasn't the dad I should have been, and now she's gone, and I can't fix it. It just makes me feel like I'm no good to any of them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I suppose I have. I don't know exactly how much, I don't really step on the scale much anymore. But my belts are definitely looser. Most days I have to force myself to just eat a few bites. Even the stuff I usually like... like, my wife makes this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Has for thirty years. She puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, gives it this nice crust. But lately, she puts it in front of me and it just sits there until I end up throwing it out. \n\nSame thing at work. I used to look forward to the lunch truck pulling up at the warehouse. The guy who runs it, Hector, he makes these amazing Cuban sandwiches. Real roasted pork, not the deli sliced stuff. Hector's a great guy. He bought that truck off his cousin about five years ago and completely gutted it, put all new stainless steel in. He was telling me the other day about how much a commercial flat top grill costs now. It's highway robbery, what they charge for restaurant equipment. You'd think it was plated in gold. And the permits? Don't even get me started on what the city makes him pay just to park the thing. \n\nBut yeah, even Hector's sandwiches... I just don't have the stomach for them right now. I just can't seem to make myself eat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. It's actually the other way around. I feel like my whole body is just... moving through mud. The guys at work keep asking me if I'm sick or something, because they see I'm walking a lot slower, and I'm taking forever just to answer simple questions. \n\nI manage the operations over at the distribution center, and usually I'm all over the floor. We've got a hundred and fifty thousand square feet of rack space. We use these Raymond reach trucks to pull the pallets down from the top racks. Great machines, really. They run on these massive industrial batteries that weigh about two thousand pounds each. You gotta use a special hoist just to swap them out at the charging station. One time, this kid on the night shift—good kid, but a little careless—he didn't hook the spreader bar up right and almost dropped one of those batteries right onto the concrete floor. The acid in those things is no joke. It'll eat right through your steel-toe boots like they're paper. You gotta wear the thick rubber apron and the face shield when you water them, OSHA requires it. The safety inspector we get, this guy Stan, he's a real stickler for that stuff. He'll write you up just for having your safety glasses resting up on your hard hat instead of over your eyes. Stan doesn't mess around. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not fidgety. Just... slow. It just takes me a long time to process what anybody is saying to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fidgety or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, doc, like I was just saying... I really don't have any trouble sitting still. It's the exact opposite. Sitting is about all I want to do right now. Like I mentioned, I just feel heavy, like I'm moving through mud. That's why the guys at work are constantly asking me if I'm coming down with a bug. I'm just dragging my feet everywhere, and if they ask me something, it takes me forever just to get the words out to answer them. \n\nTommy, our forklift lead, he was the one asking me yesterday if I had the flu. Which is pretty rich coming from him, because last winter he came in sick and took down half the morning shift. We were short-handed for a month. I had to call up this temp agency over on 4th street just to get warm bodies in the door to load the trucks. But man, the temps they send you these days... they don't have an ounce of common sense. This one kid showed up to his first shift wearing canvas sneakers. Canvas! Around five-ton machines! I told him, son, if a pallet jack clips your foot in those, they're gonna have to amputate your toes. I had to send him right back to the agency. You can't mess around with footwear in a warehouse. I've been wearing Red Wings since I was nineteen. They cost a pretty penny, but they last forever if you oil the leather right. You gotta use mink oil, not that synthetic stuff they sell at the big box stores. \n\nBut yeah... no trouble sitting still. I'm just... really slow right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you need to pace back and forth or can't stay still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. No, I don't need to pace. I don't have any problem staying still. Like I keep saying, I'm just... I'm moving like molasses. My whole body feels like it's dragging through thick mud. That's why the guys at the warehouse keep asking if I'm sick. They see me walking slower, and taking forever just to answer a simple question. \n\nSpeaking of questions, my wife asked me the other day if I thought we should get the gutters cleaned before the fall really hits. We've got these big oak trees in the front yard, beautiful trees, probably fifty, sixty years old. But man, the leaves they drop... it's a nightmare. Last year, the gutters backed up so bad the water was pouring right over the edge and washed out all her petunias. She was furious. We had this guy, Dave, who used to come out and do it for fifty bucks. He had this extendable ladder, real nice fiberglass one, not that aluminum junk that wobbles in the wind. But Dave moved down to Florida, somewhere near Tampa, I think. Said his arthritis couldn't take the winters up here anymore. I don't blame him. The cold gets into your joints as you get older. I feel it in my knees every time it rains. \n\nBut yeah... no pacing. I'm just slow. Really, really slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your feelings of worthlessness and guilt are interfering with your ability to work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they are. They really are. It's... it's getting bad at work. I've been making all these mistakes, just stupid things I'd never normally do. It got to the point where one of the upper supervisors actually found me sitting in my truck in the parking lot before dawn. I just... I couldn't bring myself to go inside. So he ended up getting occupational health involved, and now it's this whole big thing. \n\nOccupational health, they mean well, I guess. The nurse they have there, Brenda, she's been there since I started. She's got this little office right off the break room, always smells like rubbing alcohol and those stale vanilla air fresheners. Brenda's a good egg, though. She helped me out when I sliced my thumb open on a box cutter back in '98. Those box cutters, you gotta make sure you retract the blade all the way. We use the safety ones now, the ones where the blade snaps back on its own if you let go of the slider. Honestly, they're a pain to use when you're trying to break down heavy double-wall cardboard, but corporate mandated them after our workers' comp premiums went up. Insurance companies, they run everything now. You can't even change a lightbulb without filling out a risk assessment form. \n\nBut yeah, at home too, it's just falling apart. I'm letting bills pile up on the counter. Stuff I'd usually handle right away, I just look at the envelopes and I can't even bring myself to open them. It's just too much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don't... I mean, I'm not really sure if I'd call it a low point. I don't know how you'd even measure that, to be honest. 

Speaking of measuring, it's like when corporate made us swap out all the heavy-duty analog scales on the loading dock for these new digital ones last spring. They brought in this contractor, Gary, to calibrate them all. Nice enough guy, but he drove this beat-up old Ford Ranger, and the transmission was slipping so bad you could hear him backing into the lot from three streets over. I told him, I said, 'Gary, you gotta get some Lucas Transmission Fix in there.' My old man swore by that stuff. He had this '78 Chevy C10, two-tone green and white. Beautiful truck before the road salt ate through the wheel wells. We used to take it up to Miller's Pond on weekends, fishing for crappie. You gotta use live minnows if you want to catch anything decent up there, none of those plastic jigs. The bait shop we used to go to shut down a few years back, though... bought out by some big gas station chain. Shame, really, the guy who ran it knew everything about the local lakes. 

But... what were you asking? A low point? Yeah, I don't know about all that. I just know things aren't right with me.

One minute you're teaching them how to ride a bike without training wheels, and the next they're packing up their car to move across the country. This one kid showed up to his first shift wearing canvas sneakers. Canvas! Around five-ton machines! I told him, son, if a pallet jack clips your foot in those, they're gonna have to amputate your toes. I had to send him right back to the agency. You can't mess around with footwear in a warehouse. I've been wearing Red Wings since I was nineteen. They cost a pretty penny, but they last forever if you oil the leather right. You gotta use mink oil, not that synthetic stuff they sell at the big box stores. \n\nBut yeah... no trouble sitting still. I'm just... really slow right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you need to pace back and forth or can't stay still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. That's why the guys at the warehouse keep asking if I'm sick. They see me walking slower, and taking forever just to answer a simple question. \n\nSpeaking of questions, my wife asked me the other day if I thought we should get the gutters cleaned before the fall really hits. We've got these big oak trees in the front yard, beautiful trees, probably fifty, sixty years old. But man, the leaves they drop... it's a nightmare. Last year, the gutters backed up so bad the water was pouring right over the edge and washed out all her petunias. She was furious. I don't know exactly how much, I don't really step on the scale much anymore. But my belts are definitely looser. Most days I have to force myself to just eat a few bites. Even the stuff I usually like... like, my wife makes this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Has for thirty years. She puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, gives it this nice crust. But lately, she puts it in front of me and it just sits there until I end up throwing it out. \n\nSame thing at work. I used to look forward to the lunch truck pulling up at the warehouse. The guy who runs it, Hector, he makes these amazing Cuban sandwiches. Real roasted pork, not the deli sliced stuff. Hector's a great guy. He bought that truck off his cousin about five years ago and completely gutted it, put all new stainless steel in. He was telling me the other day about how much a commercial flat top grill costs now. It's highway robbery, what they charge for restaurant equipment. You'd think it was plated in gold. And the permits? Don't even get me started on what the city makes him pay just to park the thing. \n\nBut yeah, even Hector's sandwiches... I just don't have the stomach for them right now. I just can't seem to make myself eat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. It's actually the other way around. I feel like my whole body is just... moving through mud. The guys at work keep asking me if I'm sick or something, because they see I'm walking a lot slower, and I'm taking forever just to answer simple questions. \n\nI manage the operations over at the distribution center, and usually I'm all over the floor. We've got a hundred and fifty thousand square feet of rack space. We use these Raymond reach trucks to pull the pallets down from the top racks. Great machines, really. They run on these massive industrial batteries that weigh about two thousand pounds each. You gotta use a special hoist just to swap them out at the charging station. One time, this kid on the night shift—good kid, but a little careless—he didn't hook the spreader bar up right and almost dropped one of those batteries right onto the concrete floor. The acid in those things is no joke. It'll eat right through your steel-toe boots like they're paper. You gotta wear the thick rubber apron and the face shield when you water them, OSHA requires it. The safety inspector we get, this guy Stan, he's a real stickler for that stuff. He'll write you up just for having your safety glasses resting up on your hard hat instead of over your eyes. Stan doesn't mess around. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not fidgety. Just... slow. It just takes me a long time to process what anybody is saying to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fidgety or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, doc, like I was just saying... I really don't have any trouble sitting still. It's the exact opposite. Sitting is about all I want to do right now. Like I mentioned, I just feel heavy, like I'm moving through mud. That's why the guys at work are constantly asking me if I'm coming down with a bug. I'm just dragging my feet everywhere, and if they ask me something, it takes me forever just to get the words out to answer them. \n\nTommy, our forklift lead, he was the one asking me yesterday if I had the flu. Which is pretty rich coming from him, because last winter he came in sick and took down half the morning shift. We were short-handed for a month. I had to call up this temp agency over on 4th street just to get warm bodies in the door to load the trucks. But man, the temps they send you these days... they don't have an ounce of common sense. This one kid showed up to his first shift wearing canvas sneakers. Canvas! Around five-ton machines! I told him, son, if a pallet jack clips your foot in those, they're gonna have to amputate your toes. I had to send him right back to the agency. You can't mess around with footwear in a warehouse. I've been wearing Red Wings since I was nineteen. They cost a pretty penny, but they last forever if you oil the leather right. You gotta use mink oil, not that synthetic stuff they sell at the big box stores. \n\nBut yeah... no trouble sitting still. I'm just... really slow right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you need to pace back and forth or can't stay still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. Last year, the gutters backed up so bad the water was pouring right over the edge and washed out all her petunias. She was furious. It got to the point where one of the upper supervisors actually found me sitting in my truck in the parking lot before dawn. I just... I couldn't bring myself to go inside. The nurse they have there, Brenda, she's been there since I started. She's got this little office right off the break room, always smells like rubbing alcohol and those stale vanilla air fresheners. We use the safety ones now, the ones where the blade snaps back on its own if you let go of the slider. Honestly, they're a pain to use when you're trying to break down heavy double-wall cardboard, but corporate mandated them after our workers' comp premiums went up. Insurance companies, they run everything now. You can't even change a lightbulb without filling out a risk assessment form. \n\nBut yeah, at home too, it's just falling apart. I'm letting bills pile up on the counter. Stuff I'd usually handle right away, I just look at the envelopes and I can't even bring myself to open them. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're at a low point in your life right now?\n\nPatient: I don't... I mean, I'm not really sure if I'd call it a low point. Yeah, it's... it's pretty much all the time. Every day. It's just this weight, you know? I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it in my head. I keep telling myself I must have failed her somehow, like... like a better father would have done something different, said something different. 

She used to love when I'd take her to the county fair when she was little. We'd go every August, right before school started. They always had this one funnel cake stand near the Ferris wheel, the one with the blue awning, not the red one. The red one used cheap oil, you could taste it. But the blue awning guys, they powdered the sugar on so thick it looked like a snowstorm. She'd get it all over her shirt, her face... my wife used to get so mad about the laundry. We had this old top-loading Kenmore washer back then. Thing sounded like a jet engine taking off when it hit the spin cycle. I tried fixing the agitator dogs once, bought the replacement kit from a parts supply house over on 4th Street. The guy behind the counter there, old timer named Sal, he knew every appliance part number by heart. Didn't even need the computer. You don't see that kind of knowledge anymore. Everyone just looks things up on their phones now. 

But yeah, I just... I can't stop thinking about how I messed up with her. It's just always there, all day long.
