Heavy as a tank, but you could drop it off a roof and it wouldn't dent. Nowadays everything is aluminum or carbon fiber. People spend thousands of dollars on these fancy road bikes just to ride around the park on a Sunday. I see them sometimes when I drive past Memorial Park, all decked out in the tight spandex shorts like they're in the Tour de France. It's ridiculous. My neighbor, Gary, he bought one of those things. Must have cost him two grand. He rode it twice and now it just hangs in his garage next to his golf clubs. Gary's always buying stuff like that. He got a riding mower last year for a lawn that's barely bigger than a postage stamp. \n\nBut yeah... I just feel like I wasn't the dad I should have been, and now she's gone, and I can't fix it. It just makes me feel like I'm no good to any of them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I suppose I have. I don't know exactly how much, I don't really step on the scale much anymore. But my belts are definitely looser. Most days I have to force myself to just eat a few bites. Even the stuff I usually like... like, my wife makes this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Has for thirty years. She puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, gives it this nice crust. But lately, she puts it in front of me and it just sits there until I end up throwing it out. \n\nSame thing at work. I used to look forward to the lunch truck pulling up at the warehouse. The guy who runs it, Hector, he makes these amazing Cuban sandwiches. Real roasted pork, not the deli sliced stuff. Hector's a great guy. He bought that truck off his cousin about five years ago and completely gutted it, put all new stainless steel in. He was telling me the other day about how much a commercial flat top grill costs now. It's highway robbery, what they charge for restaurant equipment. You'd think it was plated in gold. And the permits? Don't even get me started on what the city makes him pay just to park the thing. \n\nBut yeah, even Hector's sandwiches... I just don't have the stomach for them right now. I just can't seem to make myself eat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. It's actually the other way around. I feel like my whole body is just... moving through mud. The guys at work keep asking me if I'm sick or something, because they see I'm walking a lot slower, and I'm taking forever just to answer simple questions. \n\nI manage the operations over at the distribution center, and usually I'm all over the floor. We've got a hundred and fifty thousand square feet of rack space. We use these Raymond reach trucks to pull the pallets down from the top racks. Great machines, really. They run on these massive industrial batteries that weigh about two thousand pounds each. You gotta use a special hoist just to swap them out at the charging station. One time, this kid on the night shift—good kid, but a little careless—he didn't hook the spreader bar up right and almost dropped one of those batteries right onto the concrete floor. The acid in those things is no joke. It'll eat right through your steel-toe boots like they're paper. You gotta wear the thick rubber apron and the face shield when you water them, OSHA requires it. The safety inspector we get, this guy Stan, he's a real stickler for that stuff. He'll write you up just for having your safety glasses resting up on your hard hat instead of over your eyes. Stan doesn't mess around. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not fidgety. Just... slow. It just takes me a long time to process what anybody is saying to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fidgety or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, doc, like I was just saying... I really don't have any trouble sitting still. It's the exact opposite. Sitting is about all I want to do right now. Like I mentioned, I just feel heavy, like I'm moving through mud. That's why the guys at work are constantly asking me if I'm coming down with a bug. I'm just dragging my feet everywhere, and if they ask me something, it takes me forever just to get the words out to answer them. \n\nTommy, our forklift lead, he was the one asking me yesterday if I had the flu. Which is pretty rich coming from him, because last winter he came in sick and took down half the morning shift. We were short-handed for a month. I had to call up this temp agency over on 4th street just to get warm bodies in the door to load the trucks. But man, the temps they send you these days... they don't have an ounce of common sense. This one kid showed up to his first shift wearing canvas sneakers. Canvas! Around five-ton machines! I told him, son, if a pallet jack clips your foot in those, they're gonna have to amputate your toes. I had to send him right back to the agency. You can't mess around with footwear in a warehouse. I've been wearing Red Wings since I was nineteen. They cost a pretty penny, but they last forever if you oil the leather right. You gotta use mink oil, not that synthetic stuff they sell at the big box stores. \n\nBut yeah... no trouble sitting still. I'm just... really slow right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you need to pace back and forth or can't stay still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. No, I don't need to pace. I don't have any problem staying still. Like I keep saying, I'm just... I'm moving like molasses. My whole body feels like it's dragging through thick mud. That's why the guys at the warehouse keep asking if I'm sick. They see me walking slower, and taking forever just to answer a simple question. \n\nSpeaking of questions, my wife asked me the other day if I thought we should get the gutters cleaned before the fall really hits. We've got these big oak trees in the front yard, beautiful trees, probably fifty, sixty years old. But man, the leaves they drop... it's a nightmare. Last year, the gutters backed up so bad the water was pouring right over the edge and washed out all her petunias. She was furious. We had this guy, Dave, who used to come out and do it for fifty bucks. He had this extendable ladder, real nice fiberglass one, not that aluminum junk that wobbles in the wind. But Dave moved down to Florida, somewhere near Tampa, I think. Said his arthritis couldn't take the winters up here anymore. I don't blame him. The cold gets into your joints as you get older. I feel it in my knees every time it rains. \n\nBut yeah... no pacing. I'm just slow. Really, really slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your feelings of worthlessness and guilt are interfering with your ability to work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they are. They really are. It's... it's getting bad at work. I've been making all these mistakes, just stupid things I'd never normally do. It got to the point where one of the upper supervisors actually found me sitting in my truck in the parking lot before dawn. I just... I couldn't bring myself to go inside. So he ended up getting occupational health involved, and now it's this whole big thing. \n\nOccupational health, they mean well, I guess. The nurse they have there, Brenda, she's been there since I started. She's got this little office right off the break room, always smells like rubbing alcohol and those stale vanilla air fresheners. Brenda's a good egg, though. She helped me out when I sliced my thumb open on a box cutter back in '98. Those box cutters, you gotta make sure you retract the blade all the way. We use the safety ones now, the ones where the blade snaps back on its own if you let go of the slider. Honestly, they're a pain to use when you're trying to break down heavy double-wall cardboard, but corporate mandated them after our workers' comp premiums went up. Insurance companies, they run everything now. You can't even change a lightbulb without filling out a risk assessment form. \n\nBut yeah, at home too, it's just falling apart. I'm letting bills pile up on the counter. Stuff I'd usually handle right away, I just look at the envelopes and I can't even bring myself to open them. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're at a low point in your life right now?\n\nPatient: I don't... I mean, I'm not really sure if I'd call it a low point. I don't know how you'd even measure that, to be honest. \n\nSpeaking of measuring, it's like when corporate made us swap out all the heavy-duty analog scales on the loading dock for these new digital ones last spring. They brought in this contractor, Gary, to calibrate them all. Nice enough guy, but he drove this beat-up old Ford Ranger, and the transmission was slipping so bad you could hear him backing into the lot from three streets over. I told him, I said, 'Gary, you gotta get some Lucas Transmission Fix in there.' My old man swore by that stuff. He had this '78 Chevy C10, two-tone green and white. Beautiful truck before the road salt ate through the wheel wells. We used to take it up to Miller's Pond on weekends, fishing for crappie. You gotta use live minnows if you want to catch anything decent up there, none of those plastic jigs. The bait shop we used to go to shut down a few years back, though... bought out by some big gas station chain. Shame, really, the guy who ran it knew everything about the local lakes. \n\nBut... what were you asking? A low point? Yeah, I don't know about all that. I just know things aren't right with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these feelings of worthlessness and guilt are affecting your daily life most of the day or nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's pretty much all the time. Every day. It's just this weight, you know? I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it in my head. I keep telling myself I must have failed her somehow, like... like a better father would have done something different, said something different. \n\nShe used to love when I'd take her to the county fair when she was little. We'd go every August, right before school started. They always had this one funnel cake stand near the Ferris wheel, the one with the blue awning, not the red one. The red one used cheap oil, you could taste it. But the blue awning guys, they powdered the sugar on so thick it looked like a snowstorm. She'd get it all over her shirt, her face... my wife used to get so mad about the laundry. We had this old top-loading Kenmore washer back then. Thing sounded like a jet engine taking off when it hit the spin cycle. I tried fixing the agitator dogs once, bought the replacement kit from a parts supply house over on 4th Street. The guy behind the counter there, old timer named Sal, he knew every appliance part number by heart. Didn't even need the computer. You don't see that kind of knowledge anymore. Everyone just looks things up on their phones now. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I can't stop thinking about how I messed up with her. It's just always there, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these feelings of worthlessness and guilt are interfering with your ability to take care of your personal needs, like eating or showering?\n\nPatient: I guess... I mean, I don't really know if it's getting in the way of that stuff. I haven't really thought about it. \n\nI do remember when we had to redo the plumbing in the master bathroom, though. That was a nightmare. The pipes were all that old galvanized steel, you know? They corrode from the inside out, so your water pressure just drops to nothing over the years. You'd turn on the shower and it was just a trickle. I had this buddy, Carl, who used to do HVAC work, but he knew plumbing too. He told me to swap it all out for PEX tubing. PEX is amazing stuff, really. You just use these crimp rings, no soldering required. Much safer, too. You hear about guys burning their houses down trying to sweat copper pipes with a blowtorch. My neighbor across the street, Dave, he nearly did that trying to fix a leak in his basement. The fire department had to come out and everything. They brought that big ladder truck, the one they usually keep at the station over by the highway. \n\nBut, uh, taking care of myself? I don't know. I guess I'm just getting by. I haven't really paid attention.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you don't care about the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been a while now. It's hard to put an exact date on it, but... I used to love messing with my tomato plants out back. Had these beautiful Cherokee Purples last year. And I'd spend hours out in the garage, just sanding wood at my bench, getting it perfectly smooth. I'd watch the Falcons games on Sundays, and stay after church talking with the folks for an hour or more. \n\nSpeaking of the garage, I need to get the tension spring on the main door looked at. It's starting to make this awful groaning noise when it rolls up. You gotta be careful with those torsion springs, though. My uncle Bob, he tried to fix his himself back in '94, I think it was. Slipped with the winding bar and nearly took his thumb clean off. Had to rush him to the ER in his old Buick LeSabre. That car was a boat, I tell you. Floated over potholes like they weren't even there. They don't make suspension like that anymore. Now you feel every pebble on the road. The city's supposed to be repaving our street next month, but they've been saying that since last fall. They brought out all those orange barrels and just left them sitting on the curb for weeks. The neighborhood kids started moving them around at night, making little obstacle courses for their bikes. \n\nBut... yeah, lately I just look at the garden, the workbench, the football games... and it's like it all belongs to somebody else. I just don't care about any of it anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I guess... if I really think about it, this whole change in me, it's been going on for about two weeks now. Right around the time my daughter left Georgia for the military. 

Georgia, you know, it gets so incredibly humid this time of year. You step outside and it's like walking into a wet blanket. I remember when we first moved down there, we had this old window AC unit in the bedroom. It rattled so loud you couldn't even hear the TV over it. I tried putting some cardboard under the edges to stop the vibrating, but it didn't do much. You really need those foam weather stripping strips for that kind of thing. You can get a roll of it at the hardware store for a couple bucks. The guy who runs the place down the street from me, Frank, he always knows exactly what aisle everything is in. He's been running that store for thirty years. Says his grandfather built the original building back in the twenties. They used actual old-growth pine for the framing back then, you know. Wood was just denser. You try driving a nail into a piece of that old pine and it'll bend right over if you don't drill a pilot hole first. 

But yeah, it's been about two weeks since she left, and I just haven't cared about much of anything since.

I'd watch the Falcons games on Sundays, and stay after church talking with the folks for an hour or more. \n\nSpeaking of the garage, I need to get the tension spring on the main door looked at. It's starting to make this awful groaning noise when it rolls up. You gotta be careful with those torsion springs, though. My uncle Bob, he tried to fix his himself back in '94, I think it was. Slipped with the winding bar and nearly took his thumb clean off. Had to rush him to the ER in his old Buick LeSabre. That car was a boat, I tell you. Floated over potholes like they weren't even there. They don't make suspension like that anymore. Now you feel every pebble on the road. I used to love messing with my tomato plants, you know? I had this whole system going with the stakes and the netting. And I'd spend hours sanding wood at my workbench out in the garage. I made this beautiful cedar chest for my wife back in, oh, must have been '98. The wood grain on that thing was just incredible. You don't see cedar like that anymore at the hardware store. Nowadays it's all that cheap pine, warps if you just look at it wrong. I remember going to three different lumber yards trying to find the right pieces for that chest. The guy at the last place, Jimmy, he had a lazy eye, but he knew his wood. Anyway, I used to do all that, and watch the Falcons on Sunday, and stay after church talking with folks. But lately... I don't know, for the past couple weeks, I just look at all that stuff and it feels like it belongs to somebody else. I just don't have the care for it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure in doing anything at all over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not a thing. Like I was saying, I used to get so much out of messing with my tomato plants. I had the Big Boys and the Early Girls, and I'd tie them up with those little green Velcro strips. And sanding wood at my bench out there... watching the Falcons, staying after church to talk with everybody... but these last two weeks, I just look at all of it and it's like... like it belongs to somebody else. I don't care about it at all. It's funny, speaking of church, the pastor's wife, Mrs. Higgins, she always brings this casserole to the potluck. It's got tater tots on top. I think she uses the name-brand ones, Ore-Ida, because they get really crispy. The store-brand ones just turn to mush, you know? I tried telling my wife that once when she bought the cheap ones, but she didn't want to hear it. Anyway, yeah... no pleasure in anything. Just feeling empty.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or slowed down in your movements over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Slowed down? Yeah, that's exactly it. It feels like my whole body is just moving through thick mud. Even the guys at work are noticing. They keep coming up to me, asking if I'm coming down with a bug or if I'm sick. I manage the floor over at the parts warehouse, and usually I'm walking laps around that place, keeping everyone on their toes. We got this one kid on the forklift, Tommy. He's barely twenty, drives that thing like it's a go-kart. I told him a hundred times he's gonna take down a whole pallet rack if he takes the corners that fast. Back in the day, if you drove a lift like that, the foreman would have your badge before lunch. They didn't mess around with safety back in the 80s, I'll tell you that. You wore your hard hat or you went home without pay. But anyway... yeah, Tommy and the other guys, they keep asking if I'm okay because I'm just dragging my feet. I'm walking so much slower than usual. And when they ask me simple questions, like about the shipping logs or where to put a crate, it just takes me forever to get the answer out. My brain just can't find the words fast enough. It's just... mud. Everything is mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would harm yourself if you were to act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't really thought about *how* I'd do it. It's not like I'm sitting there making a plan with ropes or pills or anything like that. It's more just... this heavy feeling, you know? Like I said, I just keep having these thoughts that my wife and kids would be better off if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. That's actually why I'm here. It spooked me so bad I finally said something to my supervisor, Dave, down at the warehouse. Dave's a good guy. He's been there almost as long as I have. We started back when the company was still owned by old man Miller. Miller used to come down to the floor every Christmas and hand out turkeys to everyone. Real turkeys, not those frozen butterball things they give you coupons for nowadays. He'd shake your hand and ask about your family. You don't see bosses doing that anymore. Now it's all corporate guys in suits who wouldn't know a pallet jack from a hole in the ground. They came in last year and tried to change the whole inventory system. A disaster, complete disaster. But anyway, yeah, I told Dave about the thoughts, and he's the one who said I needed to come get checked out. So no, no plan to actually hurt myself, just wishing I wouldn't wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about having these thoughts that your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... I mean, mostly I just feel like I'm no good to them. I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it in my head, and telling myself I must have failed her somehow. Like a better father would have done something different, you know? Said the right thing, or been there more. But she just packed up her little Honda Civic and drove out to Oregon. Portland, I think it was. \n\nIt rains a lot out there. I made sure to check her tires before she hit the road. You gotta have good tread if you're driving out to the Pacific Northwest. I put a set of Michelin Defenders on there for her. You can't cheap out on tires. People buy those budget brands at the big box stores to save fifty bucks, and next thing they know they're hydroplaning into a guardrail. My buddy Sal, he runs a tire shop off Route 9, and he says he sees it all the time. Sal's a good guy. He's got this old basset hound that just sleeps right under the hydraulic lift all day. Doesn't even flinch when the air tools kick on. Just snores right through it. \n\nBut anyway... yeah. I just sit around thinking I failed her, and that they'd all just be doing better if I wasn't in the picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? I guess that's the word for it. It all just comes back to my daughter, really. I just keep replaying her leaving in my head over and over. I just sit there telling myself I must have failed her somehow, you know? Like if I was a better father, I would have done something different, said something different to make her stay. Or maybe just been there more when she was younger. \n\nKids grow up so fast. One minute you're teaching them how to ride a bike without training wheels, and the next they're packing up their car to move across the country. I taught both my kids to ride on this old Schwinn I picked up at a garage sale. Best ten bucks I ever spent. They don't make bikes like that anymore, all solid steel frame. Heavy as a tank, but you could drop it off a roof and it wouldn't dent. Nowadays everything is aluminum or carbon fiber. People spend thousands of dollars on these fancy road bikes just to ride around the park on a Sunday. I see them sometimes when I drive past Memorial Park, all decked out in the tight spandex shorts like they're in the Tour de France. It's ridiculous. My neighbor, Gary, he bought one of those things. Must have cost him two grand. He rode it twice and now it just hangs in his garage next to his golf clubs. Gary's always buying stuff like that. He got a riding mower last year for a lawn that's barely bigger than a postage stamp. \n\nBut yeah... I just feel like I wasn't the dad I should have been, and now she's gone, and I can't fix it. It just makes me feel like I'm no good to any of them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I suppose I have. I don't know exactly how much, I don't really step on the scale much anymore. But my belts are definitely looser. Most days I have to force myself to just eat a few bites. Even the stuff I usually like... like, my wife makes this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Has for thirty years. She puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, gives it this nice crust. But lately, she puts it in front of me and it just sits there until I end up throwing it out. \n\nSame thing at work. I used to look forward to the lunch truck pulling up at the warehouse. The guy who runs it, Hector, he makes these amazing Cuban sandwiches. Real roasted pork, not the deli sliced stuff. Hector's a great guy. He bought that truck off his cousin about five years ago and completely gutted it, put all new stainless steel in. He was telling me the other day about how much a commercial flat top grill costs now. It's highway robbery, what they charge for restaurant equipment. You'd think it was plated in gold. And the permits? Don't even get me started on what the city makes him pay just to park the thing. \n\nBut yeah, even Hector's sandwiches... I just don't have the stomach for them right now. I just can't seem to make myself eat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. It's actually the other way around. I feel like my whole body is just... moving through mud. The guys at work keep asking me if I'm sick or something, because they see I'm walking a lot slower, and I'm taking forever just to answer simple questions. \n\nI manage the operations over at the distribution center, and usually I'm all over the floor. We've got a hundred and fifty thousand square feet of rack space. We use these Raymond reach trucks to pull the pallets down from the top racks. Great machines, really. They run on these massive industrial batteries that weigh about two thousand pounds each. You gotta use a special hoist just to swap them out at the charging station. One time, this kid on the night shift—good kid, but a little careless—he didn't hook the spreader bar up right and almost dropped one of those batteries right onto the concrete floor. The acid in those things is no joke. It'll eat right through your steel-toe boots like they're paper. You gotta wear the thick rubber apron and the face shield when you water them, OSHA requires it. The safety inspector we get, this guy Stan, he's a real stickler for that stuff. He'll write you up just for having your safety glasses resting up on your hard hat instead of over your eyes. Stan doesn't mess around. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not fidgety. Just... slow. It just takes me a long time to process what anybody is saying to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fidgety or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, doc, like I was just saying... I really don't have any trouble sitting still. It's the exact opposite. Sitting is about all I want to do right now. Like I mentioned, I just feel heavy, like I'm moving through mud. That's why the guys at work are constantly asking me if I'm coming down with a bug. I'm just dragging my feet everywhere, and if they ask me something, it takes me forever just to get the words out to answer them. \n\nTommy, our forklift lead, he was the one asking me yesterday if I had the flu. Which is pretty rich coming from him, because last winter he came in sick and took down half the morning shift. We were short-handed for a month. I had to call up this temp agency over on 4th street just to get warm bodies in the door to load the trucks. But man, the temps they send you these days... they don't have an ounce of common sense. This one kid showed up to his first shift wearing canvas sneakers. Canvas! Around five-ton machines! I told him, son, if a pallet jack clips your foot in those, they're gonna have to amputate your toes. I had to send him right back to the agency. You can't mess around with footwear in a warehouse. I've been wearing Red Wings since I was nineteen. They cost a pretty penny, but they last forever if you oil the leather right. You gotta use mink oil, not that synthetic stuff they sell at the big box stores. \n\nBut yeah... no trouble sitting still. I'm just... really slow right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you need to pace back and forth or can't stay still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. No, I don't need to pace. I don't have any problem staying still. Like I keep saying, I'm just... I'm moving like molasses. My whole body feels like it's dragging through thick mud. That's why the guys at the warehouse keep asking if I'm sick. They see me walking slower, and taking forever just to answer a simple question. \n\nSpeaking of questions, my wife asked me the other day if I thought we should get the gutters cleaned before the fall really hits. We've got these big oak trees in the front yard, beautiful trees, probably fifty, sixty years old. But man, the leaves they drop... it's a nightmare. Last year, the gutters backed up so bad the water was pouring right over the edge and washed out all her petunias. She was furious. We had this guy, Dave, who used to come out and do it for fifty bucks. He had this extendable ladder, real nice fiberglass one, not that aluminum junk that wobbles in the wind. But Dave moved down to Florida, somewhere near Tampa, I think. Said his arthritis couldn't take the winters up here anymore. I don't blame him. The cold gets into your joints as you get older. I feel it in my knees every time it rains. \n\nBut yeah... no pacing. I'm just slow. Really, really slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your feelings of worthlessness and guilt are interfering with your ability to work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they are. They really are. It's... it's getting bad at work. I've been making all these mistakes, just stupid things I'd never normally do. It got to the point where one of the upper supervisors actually found me sitting in my truck in the parking lot before dawn. I just... I couldn't bring myself to go inside. So he ended up getting occupational health involved, and now it's this whole big thing. \n\nOccupational health, they mean well, I guess. The nurse they have there, Brenda, she's been there since I started. She's got this little office right off the break room, always smells like rubbing alcohol and those stale vanilla air fresheners. Brenda's a good egg, though. She helped me out when I sliced my thumb open on a box cutter back in '98. Those box cutters, you gotta make sure you retract the blade all the way. We use the safety ones now, the ones where the blade snaps back on its own if you let go of the slider. Honestly, they're a pain to use when you're trying to break down heavy double-wall cardboard, but corporate mandated them after our workers' comp premiums went up. Insurance companies, they run everything now. You can't even change a lightbulb without filling out a risk assessment form. \n\nBut yeah, at home too, it's just falling apart. I'm letting bills pile up on the counter. Stuff I'd usually handle right away, I just look at the envelopes and I can't even bring myself to open them. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're at a low point in your life right now?\n\nPatient: I don't... I mean, I'm not really sure if I'd call it a low point. I don't know how you'd even measure that, to be honest. \n\nSpeaking of measuring, it's like when corporate made us swap out all the heavy-duty analog scales on the loading dock for these new digital ones last spring. They brought in this contractor, Gary, to calibrate them all. Nice enough guy, but he drove this beat-up old Ford Ranger, and the transmission was slipping so bad you could hear him backing into the lot from three streets over. I told him, I said, 'Gary, you gotta get some Lucas Transmission Fix in there.' My old man swore by that stuff. He had this '78 Chevy C10, two-tone green and white. Beautiful truck before the road salt ate through the wheel wells. We used to take it up to Miller's Pond on weekends, fishing for crappie. You gotta use live minnows if you want to catch anything decent up there, none of those plastic jigs. The bait shop we used to go to shut down a few years back, though... bought out by some big gas station chain. Shame, really, the guy who ran it knew everything about the local lakes. \n\nBut... what were you asking? A low point? Yeah, I don't know about all that. I just know things aren't right with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these feelings of worthlessness and guilt are affecting your daily life most of the day or nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's pretty much all the time. Every day. It's just this weight, you know? I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it in my head. I keep telling myself I must have failed her somehow, like... like a better father would have done something different, said something different. \n\nShe used to love when I'd take her to the county fair when she was little. We'd go every August, right before school started. They always had this one funnel cake stand near the Ferris wheel, the one with the blue awning, not the red one. The red one used cheap oil, you could taste it. But the blue awning guys, they powdered the sugar on so thick it looked like a snowstorm. She'd get it all over her shirt, her face... my wife used to get so mad about the laundry. We had this old top-loading Kenmore washer back then. Thing sounded like a jet engine taking off when it hit the spin cycle. I tried fixing the agitator dogs once, bought the replacement kit from a parts supply house over on 4th Street. The guy behind the counter there, old timer named Sal, he knew every appliance part number by heart. Didn't even need the computer. You don't see that kind of knowledge anymore. Everyone just looks things up on their phones now. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I can't stop thinking about how I messed up with her. It's just always there, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these feelings of worthlessness and guilt are interfering with your ability to take care of your personal needs, like eating or showering?\n\nPatient: I guess... I mean, I don't really know if it's getting in the way of that stuff. I haven't really thought about it. \n\nI do remember when we had to redo the plumbing in the master bathroom, though. That was a nightmare. The pipes were all that old galvanized steel, you know? They corrode from the inside out, so your water pressure just drops to nothing over the years. You'd turn on the shower and it was just a trickle. I had this buddy, Carl, who used to do HVAC work, but he knew plumbing too. He told me to swap it all out for PEX tubing. PEX is amazing stuff, really. You just use these crimp rings, no soldering required. Much safer, too. You hear about guys burning their houses down trying to sweat copper pipes with a blowtorch. My neighbor across the street, Dave, he nearly did that trying to fix a leak in his basement. The fire department had to come out and everything. They brought that big ladder truck, the one they usually keep at the station over by the highway. \n\nBut, uh, taking care of myself? I don't know. I guess I'm just getting by. I haven't really paid attention.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you don't care about the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been a while now. It's hard to put an exact date on it, but... I used to love messing with my tomato plants out back. Had these beautiful Cherokee Purples last year. And I'd spend hours out in the garage, just sanding wood at my bench, getting it perfectly smooth. I'd watch the Falcons games on Sundays, and stay after church talking with the folks for an hour or more. \n\nSpeaking of the garage, I need to get the tension spring on the main door looked at. It's starting to make this awful groaning noise when it rolls up. You gotta be careful with those torsion springs, though. My uncle Bob, he tried to fix his himself back in '94, I think it was. Slipped with the winding bar and nearly took his thumb clean off. Had to rush him to the ER in his old Buick LeSabre. That car was a boat, I tell you. Floated over potholes like they weren't even there. They don't make suspension like that anymore. Now you feel every pebble on the road. The city's supposed to be repaving our street next month, but they've been saying that since last fall. They brought out all those orange barrels and just left them sitting on the curb for weeks. The neighborhood kids started moving them around at night, making little obstacle courses for their bikes. \n\nBut... yeah, lately I just look at the garden, the workbench, the football games... and it's like it all belongs to somebody else. I just don't care about any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you don't care about the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... if I really think about it, this whole change in me, it's been going on for about two weeks now. Right around the time my daughter left Georgia for the military. \n\nGeorgia, you know, it gets so incredibly humid this time of year. You step outside and it's like walking into a wet blanket. I remember when we first moved down there, we had this old window AC unit in the bedroom. It rattled so loud you couldn't even hear the TV over it. I tried putting some cardboard under the edges to stop the vibrating, but it didn't do much. You really need those foam weather stripping strips for that kind of thing. You can get a roll of it at the hardware store for a couple bucks. The guy who runs the place down the street from me, Frank, he always knows exactly what aisle everything is in. He's been running that store for thirty years. Says his grandfather built the original building back in the twenties. They used actual old-growth pine for the framing back then, you know. Wood was just denser. You try driving a nail into a piece of that old pine and it'll bend right over if you don't drill a pilot hole first. \n\nBut yeah, it's been about two weeks since she left, and I just haven't cared about much of anything since.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, not really... it's actually the opposite. I feel like my body is just moving through mud. People at work keep asking me if I'm sick because I'm walking so much slower than usual. It's taking me forever just to answer simple questions. 

Speaking of work, we had this massive shipment of corrugated boxes come in last Tuesday. They were supposed to be the heavy-duty double-wall kind, you know, for shipping automotive parts. But the supplier messed up and sent single-wall. You try putting a 40-pound brake rotor in a single-wall box, the bottom just falls right out. He's got this old Honda Civic that he's always tinkering with in the parking lot during his lunch break. Put an aftermarket exhaust on it that sounds like a swarm of angry bees. I don't get why these young guys want their cars to be so loud. Back in my day, if your car sounded like that, it meant you blew a hole in your muffler and you needed to get to Midas before you got a ticket. 

But yeah, I don't feel like moving around much at all. Just everything feels so slow and heavy.

I haven't really paid attention.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you don't care about the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been a while now. It's hard to put an exact date on it, but... I used to love messing with my tomato plants out back. Had these beautiful Cherokee Purples last year. And I'd spend hours out in the garage, just sanding wood at my bench, getting it perfectly smooth. I'd watch the Falcons games on Sundays, and stay after church talking with the folks for an hour or more. \n\nSpeaking of the garage, I need to get the tension spring on the main door looked at. It's starting to make this awful groaning noise when it rolls up. You gotta be careful with those torsion springs, though. My uncle Bob, he tried to fix his himself back in '94, I think it was. Slipped with the winding bar and nearly took his thumb clean off. Had to rush him to the ER in his old Buick LeSabre. That car was a boat, I tell you. Floated over potholes like they weren't even there. They don't make suspension like that anymore. Now you feel every pebble on the road. The city's supposed to be repaving our street next month, but they've been saying that since last fall. They brought out all those orange barrels and just left them sitting on the curb for weeks. I tried putting some cardboard under the edges to stop the vibrating, but it didn't do much. You really need those foam weather stripping strips for that kind of thing. You can get a roll of it at the hardware store for a couple bucks. The guy who runs the place down the street from me, Frank, he always knows exactly what aisle everything is in. He's been running that store for thirty years. Says his grandfather built the original building back in the twenties. They used actual old-growth pine for the framing back then, you know. Wood was just denser. You try driving a nail into a piece of that old pine and it'll bend right over if you don't drill a pilot hole first. \n\nBut yeah, it's been about two weeks since she left, and I just haven't cared about much of anything since.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you need to move around or do something physical to relieve your emotional state?\n\nPatient: No, not really... it's actually the opposite. It's taking me forever just to answer simple questions. \n\nSpeaking of work, we had this massive shipment of corrugated boxes come in last Tuesday. I don't get why these young guys want their cars to be so loud. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, it's every single day. I just... I can't get it out of my head. I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it, telling myself I must have failed her somehow. Like, if I was a better father, I would have done something different and she wouldn't have felt the need to pack up and join the military. 

The military, man... they sent her down to Texas for basic. San Antonio, I think. It gets hotter than a furnace down there. I drove through Texas back in the late eighties, hauling a load of lumber before I moved into management. Had this old Peterbilt cab-over. No AC, just the vent windows cranked open, blowing hot air in your face like a hair dryer. We used to stop at this diner right off I-10... I can't remember the name of it, but they had the best chicken fried steak. Plate as big as a hubcap, smothered in that white pepper gravy. The waitress there, she had this big hair, you know, like they wore back then, sprayed stiff as a board. She told me her husband was a mechanic for John Deere. Those old John Deere tractors, the green and yellow ones, they run forever if you keep the oil clean. You gotta check the filters, though. People forget about the fuel filters and then wonder why the engine starts sputtering when they're trying to bush hog a field. It's just basic maintenance, you know? You take care of the machine, it takes care of you. 

But... what was I saying? Oh. Yeah. Just sitting at my desk at work, staring at the shipping manifests, and all I can think about is how I messed up with her. It just eats up my whole day.

I was just talking to him—he's a good guy, you know, we've worked together for about eight years now, back when the old warehouse was still open on 4th street. That place was a nightmare to heat in the winter, let me tell you. We used to have these space heaters running all the time, which was probably a fire hazard, but what can you do? Anyway, we were talking, and I just sort of let it slip that... well, lately I've been having these thoughts. Just... thinking that maybe my family would be better off if I just didn't wake up in the morning. It kind of scared me to say it out loud, and it definitely spooked him, so... here I am.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been having these thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess this whole change in me... it’s been going on for about two weeks now. It started right around the time my daughter left Georgia for the military. She actually drove down there herself. I told her to make sure she checked the oil and the tire pressure before she hit the highway, you know? Kids these days, they just get in and drive. They don't think about the belts or the hoses or anything. I remember back in '92, I had this old Ford truck, and the radiator hose blew right in the middle of a bridge during rush hour. Steam going everywhere, people honking. I had to walk three miles to a gas station in the pouring rain just to get a jug of coolant. Ruined my favorite pair of work boots, too. Had to throw 'em right in the trash. But anyway... yeah, since she packed up and left for the army two weeks ago, that's when I really started having those thoughts about them not needing me around.\n\nDoctor: Have you been enjoying the things you usually like to do over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, no, not really. I used to love messing with my tomato plants, you know? I had this whole system going with the stakes and the netting. And I'd spend hours sanding wood at my workbench out in the garage. I made this beautiful cedar chest for my wife back in, oh, must have been '98. The wood grain on that thing was just incredible. You don't see cedar like that anymore at the hardware store. Nowadays it's all that cheap pine, warps if you just look at it wrong. I remember going to three different lumber yards trying to find the right pieces for that chest. The guy at the last place, Jimmy, he had a lazy eye, but he knew his wood. Anyway, I used to do all that, and watch the Falcons on Sunday, and stay after church talking with folks. But lately... I don't know, for the past couple weeks, I just look at all that stuff and it feels like it belongs to somebody else. I just don't have the care for it right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt much pleasure in doing anything at all over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, not a thing. Like I was saying, I used to get so much out of messing with my tomato plants. I had the Big Boys and the Early Girls, and I'd tie them up with those little green Velcro strips. And sanding wood at my bench out there... watching the Falcons, staying after church to talk with everybody... but these last two weeks, I just look at all of it and it's like... like it belongs to somebody else. I don't care about it at all. It's funny, speaking of church, the pastor's wife, Mrs. Higgins, she always brings this casserole to the potluck. It's got tater tots on top. I think she uses the name-brand ones, Ore-Ida, because they get really crispy. The store-brand ones just turn to mush, you know? I tried telling my wife that once when she bought the cheap ones, but she didn't want to hear it. Anyway, yeah... no pleasure in anything. Just feeling empty.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or slowed down in your movements over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Slowed down? Yeah, that's exactly it. It feels like my whole body is just moving through thick mud. Even the guys at work are noticing. They keep coming up to me, asking if I'm coming down with a bug or if I'm sick. I manage the floor over at the parts warehouse, and usually I'm walking laps around that place, keeping everyone on their toes. We got this one kid on the forklift, Tommy. He's barely twenty, drives that thing like it's a go-kart. I told him a hundred times he's gonna take down a whole pallet rack if he takes the corners that fast. Back in the day, if you drove a lift like that, the foreman would have your badge before lunch. They didn't mess around with safety back in the 80s, I'll tell you that. You wore your hard hat or you went home without pay. But anyway... yeah, Tommy and the other guys, they keep asking if I'm okay because I'm just dragging my feet. I'm walking so much slower than usual. And when they ask me simple questions, like about the shipping logs or where to put a crate, it just takes me forever to get the answer out. My brain just can't find the words fast enough. It's just... mud. Everything is mud.\n\nDoctor: Have you thought about how you would harm yourself if you were to act on those thoughts about your family being better off without you?\n\nPatient: Well, no, I haven't really thought about *how* I'd do it. It's not like I'm sitting there making a plan with ropes or pills or anything like that. It's more just... this heavy feeling, you know? Like I said, I just keep having these thoughts that my wife and kids would be better off if I just went to sleep and didn't wake up. That's actually why I'm here. It spooked me so bad I finally said something to my supervisor, Dave, down at the warehouse. Dave's a good guy. He's been there almost as long as I have. We started back when the company was still owned by old man Miller. Miller used to come down to the floor every Christmas and hand out turkeys to everyone. Real turkeys, not those frozen butterball things they give you coupons for nowadays. He'd shake your hand and ask about your family. You don't see bosses doing that anymore. Now it's all corporate guys in suits who wouldn't know a pallet jack from a hole in the ground. They came in last year and tried to change the whole inventory system. A disaster, complete disaster. But anyway, yeah, I told Dave about the thoughts, and he's the one who said I needed to come get checked out. So no, no plan to actually hurt myself, just wishing I wouldn't wake up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about having these thoughts that your family would be better off without you?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Yeah... I mean, mostly I just feel like I'm no good to them. I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it in my head, and telling myself I must have failed her somehow. Like a better father would have done something different, you know? Said the right thing, or been there more. But she just packed up her little Honda Civic and drove out to Oregon. Portland, I think it was. \n\nIt rains a lot out there. I made sure to check her tires before she hit the road. You gotta have good tread if you're driving out to the Pacific Northwest. I put a set of Michelin Defenders on there for her. You can't cheap out on tires. People buy those budget brands at the big box stores to save fifty bucks, and next thing they know they're hydroplaning into a guardrail. My buddy Sal, he runs a tire shop off Route 9, and he says he sees it all the time. Sal's a good guy. He's got this old basset hound that just sleeps right under the hydraulic lift all day. Doesn't even flinch when the air tools kick on. Just snores right through it. \n\nBut anyway... yeah. I just sit around thinking I failed her, and that they'd all just be doing better if I wasn't in the picture.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? I guess that's the word for it. It all just comes back to my daughter, really. I just keep replaying her leaving in my head over and over. I just sit there telling myself I must have failed her somehow, you know? Like if I was a better father, I would have done something different, said something different to make her stay. Or maybe just been there more when she was younger. \n\nKids grow up so fast. One minute you're teaching them how to ride a bike without training wheels, and the next they're packing up their car to move across the country. I taught both my kids to ride on this old Schwinn I picked up at a garage sale. Best ten bucks I ever spent. They don't make bikes like that anymore, all solid steel frame. Heavy as a tank, but you could drop it off a roof and it wouldn't dent. Nowadays everything is aluminum or carbon fiber. People spend thousands of dollars on these fancy road bikes just to ride around the park on a Sunday. I see them sometimes when I drive past Memorial Park, all decked out in the tight spandex shorts like they're in the Tour de France. It's ridiculous. My neighbor, Gary, he bought one of those things. Must have cost him two grand. He rode it twice and now it just hangs in his garage next to his golf clubs. Gary's always buying stuff like that. He got a riding mower last year for a lawn that's barely bigger than a postage stamp. \n\nBut yeah... I just feel like I wasn't the dad I should have been, and now she's gone, and I can't fix it. It just makes me feel like I'm no good to any of them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Weight? Yeah, I suppose I have. I don't know exactly how much, I don't really step on the scale much anymore. But my belts are definitely looser. Most days I have to force myself to just eat a few bites. Even the stuff I usually like... like, my wife makes this incredible meatloaf on Tuesdays. Has for thirty years. She puts a little brown sugar in the ketchup glaze, gives it this nice crust. But lately, she puts it in front of me and it just sits there until I end up throwing it out. \n\nSame thing at work. I used to look forward to the lunch truck pulling up at the warehouse. The guy who runs it, Hector, he makes these amazing Cuban sandwiches. Real roasted pork, not the deli sliced stuff. Hector's a great guy. He bought that truck off his cousin about five years ago and completely gutted it, put all new stainless steel in. He was telling me the other day about how much a commercial flat top grill costs now. It's highway robbery, what they charge for restaurant equipment. You'd think it was plated in gold. And the permits? Don't even get me started on what the city makes him pay just to park the thing. \n\nBut yeah, even Hector's sandwiches... I just don't have the stomach for them right now. I just can't seem to make myself eat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or fidgety over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Restless? No, not really. It's actually the other way around. I feel like my whole body is just... moving through mud. One time, this kid on the night shift—good kid, but a little careless—he didn't hook the spreader bar up right and almost dropped one of those batteries right onto the concrete floor. The acid in those things is no joke. It'll eat right through your steel-toe boots like they're paper. You gotta wear the thick rubber apron and the face shield when you water them, OSHA requires it. The safety inspector we get, this guy Stan, he's a real stickler for that stuff. He'll write you up just for having your safety glasses resting up on your hard hat instead of over your eyes. Stan doesn't mess around. \n\nBut anyway... no, I'm not fidgety. Just... slow. It just takes me a long time to process what anybody is saying to me lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling fidgety or having trouble sitting still over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, doc, like I was just saying... I really don't have any trouble sitting still. It's the exact opposite. Sitting is about all I want to do right now. Like I mentioned, I just feel heavy, like I'm moving through mud. That's why the guys at work are constantly asking me if I'm coming down with a bug. I'm just dragging my feet everywhere, and if they ask me something, it takes me forever just to get the words out to answer them. \n\nTommy, our forklift lead, he was the one asking me yesterday if I had the flu. Which is pretty rich coming from him, because last winter he came in sick and took down half the morning shift. We were short-handed for a month. I'm just... really slow right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you need to pace back and forth or can't stay still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. No, I don't need to pace. I don't have any problem staying still. Like I keep saying, I'm just... I'm moving like molasses. My whole body feels like it's dragging through thick mud. That's why the guys at the warehouse keep asking if I'm sick. They see me walking slower, and taking forever just to answer a simple question. \n\nSpeaking of questions, my wife asked me the other day if I thought we should get the gutters cleaned before the fall really hits. We've got these big oak trees in the front yard, beautiful trees, probably fifty, sixty years old. But man, the leaves they drop... it's a nightmare. Last year, the gutters backed up so bad the water was pouring right over the edge and washed out all her petunias. She was furious. But Dave moved down to Florida, somewhere near Tampa, I think. Said his arthritis couldn't take the winters up here anymore. I don't blame him. The cold gets into your joints as you get older. I feel it in my knees every time it rains. \n\nBut yeah... no pacing. I'm just slow. Really, really slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your feelings of worthlessness and guilt are interfering with your ability to work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they are. They really are. It's... it's getting bad at work. I've been making all these mistakes, just stupid things I'd never normally do. It got to the point where one of the upper supervisors actually found me sitting in my truck in the parking lot before dawn. I just... I couldn't bring myself to go inside. So he ended up getting occupational health involved, and now it's this whole big thing. \n\nOccupational health, they mean well, I guess. The nurse they have there, Brenda, she's been there since I started. She's got this little office right off the break room, always smells like rubbing alcohol and those stale vanilla air fresheners. Brenda's a good egg, though. She helped me out when I sliced my thumb open on a box cutter back in '98. Those box cutters, you gotta make sure you retract the blade all the way. Honestly, they're a pain to use when you're trying to break down heavy double-wall cardboard, but corporate mandated them after our workers' comp premiums went up. Insurance companies, they run everything now. You can't even change a lightbulb without filling out a risk assessment form. \n\nBut yeah, at home too, it's just falling apart. I'm letting bills pile up on the counter. Stuff I'd usually handle right away, I just look at the envelopes and I can't even bring myself to open them. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're at a low point in your life right now?\n\nPatient: I don't... I mean, I'm not really sure if I'd call it a low point. I don't know how you'd even measure that, to be honest. \n\nSpeaking of measuring, it's like when corporate made us swap out all the heavy-duty analog scales on the loading dock for these new digital ones last spring. You gotta use live minnows if you want to catch anything decent up there, none of those plastic jigs. I really don't have any trouble sitting still. It's the exact opposite. Sitting is about all I want to do right now. Like I mentioned, I just feel heavy, like I'm moving through mud. That's why the guys at work are constantly asking me if I'm coming down with a bug. I'm just dragging my feet everywhere, and if they ask me something, it takes me forever just to get the words out to answer them. \n\nTommy, our forklift lead, he was the one asking me yesterday if I had the flu. Which is pretty rich coming from him, because last winter he came in sick and took down half the morning shift. We were short-handed for a month. I had to call up this temp agency over on 4th street just to get warm bodies in the door to load the trucks. But man, the temps they send you these days... they don't have an ounce of common sense. This one kid showed up to his first shift wearing canvas sneakers. Canvas! Around five-ton machines! I told him, son, if a pallet jack clips your foot in those, they're gonna have to amputate your toes. I had to send him right back to the agency. You can't mess around with footwear in a warehouse. I've been wearing Red Wings since I was nineteen. They cost a pretty penny, but they last forever if you oil the leather right. You gotta use mink oil, not that synthetic stuff they sell at the big box stores. \n\nBut yeah... no trouble sitting still. I'm just... really slow right now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you need to pace back and forth or can't stay still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: Doc, I feel like we're going in circles here. No, I don't need to pace. I don't have any problem staying still. Like I keep saying, I'm just... I'm moving like molasses. My whole body feels like it's dragging through thick mud. That's why the guys at the warehouse keep asking if I'm sick. They see me walking slower, and taking forever just to answer a simple question. \n\nSpeaking of questions, my wife asked me the other day if I thought we should get the gutters cleaned before the fall really hits. We've got these big oak trees in the front yard, beautiful trees, probably fifty, sixty years old. But man, the leaves they drop... it's a nightmare. Last year, the gutters backed up so bad the water was pouring right over the edge and washed out all her petunias. She was furious. We had this guy, Dave, who used to come out and do it for fifty bucks. He had this extendable ladder, real nice fiberglass one, not that aluminum junk that wobbles in the wind. But Dave moved down to Florida, somewhere near Tampa, I think. Said his arthritis couldn't take the winters up here anymore. I don't blame him. The cold gets into your joints as you get older. I feel it in my knees every time it rains. \n\nBut yeah... no pacing. I'm just slow. Really, really slow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your feelings of worthlessness and guilt are interfering with your ability to work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, they are. They really are. It's... it's getting bad at work. I've been making all these mistakes, just stupid things I'd never normally do. It got to the point where one of the upper supervisors actually found me sitting in my truck in the parking lot before dawn. I just... I couldn't bring myself to go inside. So he ended up getting occupational health involved, and now it's this whole big thing. \n\nOccupational health, they mean well, I guess. The nurse they have there, Brenda, she's been there since I started. She's got this little office right off the break room, always smells like rubbing alcohol and those stale vanilla air fresheners. Brenda's a good egg, though. She helped me out when I sliced my thumb open on a box cutter back in '98. Those box cutters, you gotta make sure you retract the blade all the way. We use the safety ones now, the ones where the blade snaps back on its own if you let go of the slider. Honestly, they're a pain to use when you're trying to break down heavy double-wall cardboard, but corporate mandated them after our workers' comp premiums went up. Insurance companies, they run everything now. You can't even change a lightbulb without filling out a risk assessment form. \n\nBut yeah, at home too, it's just falling apart. I'm letting bills pile up on the counter. Stuff I'd usually handle right away, I just look at the envelopes and I can't even bring myself to open them. It's just too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you're at a low point in your life right now?\n\nPatient: I don't... I mean, I'm not really sure if I'd call it a low point. I don't know how you'd even measure that, to be honest. \n\nSpeaking of measuring, it's like when corporate made us swap out all the heavy-duty analog scales on the loading dock for these new digital ones last spring. They brought in this contractor, Gary, to calibrate them all. Nice enough guy, but he drove this beat-up old Ford Ranger, and the transmission was slipping so bad you could hear him backing into the lot from three streets over. I told him, I said, 'Gary, you gotta get some Lucas Transmission Fix in there.' My old man swore by that stuff. He had this '78 Chevy C10, two-tone green and white. Beautiful truck before the road salt ate through the wheel wells. We used to take it up to Miller's Pond on weekends, fishing for crappie. You gotta use live minnows if you want to catch anything decent up there, none of those plastic jigs. The bait shop we used to go to shut down a few years back, though... bought out by some big gas station chain. Shame, really, the guy who ran it knew everything about the local lakes. \n\nBut... what were you asking? A low point? Yeah, I don't know about all that. I just know things aren't right with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these feelings of worthlessness and guilt are affecting your daily life most of the day or nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's... it's pretty much all the time. Every day. It's just this weight, you know? I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it in my head. I keep telling myself I must have failed her somehow, like... like a better father would have done something different, said something different. \n\nShe used to love when I'd take her to the county fair when she was little. We'd go every August, right before school started. They always had this one funnel cake stand near the Ferris wheel, the one with the blue awning, not the red one. The red one used cheap oil, you could taste it. But the blue awning guys, they powdered the sugar on so thick it looked like a snowstorm. She'd get it all over her shirt, her face... my wife used to get so mad about the laundry. We had this old top-loading Kenmore washer back then. Thing sounded like a jet engine taking off when it hit the spin cycle. I tried fixing the agitator dogs once, bought the replacement kit from a parts supply house over on 4th Street. The guy behind the counter there, old timer named Sal, he knew every appliance part number by heart. Didn't even need the computer. You don't see that kind of knowledge anymore. Everyone just looks things up on their phones now. \n\nBut yeah, I just... I can't stop thinking about how I messed up with her. It's just always there, all day long.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these feelings of worthlessness and guilt are interfering with your ability to take care of your personal needs, like eating or showering?\n\nPatient: I guess... I mean, I don't really know if it's getting in the way of that stuff. I haven't really thought about it. \n\nI do remember when we had to redo the plumbing in the master bathroom, though. That was a nightmare. The pipes were all that old galvanized steel, you know? They corrode from the inside out, so your water pressure just drops to nothing over the years. You'd turn on the shower and it was just a trickle. I had this buddy, Carl, who used to do HVAC work, but he knew plumbing too. He told me to swap it all out for PEX tubing. PEX is amazing stuff, really. You just use these crimp rings, no soldering required. Much safer, too. You hear about guys burning their houses down trying to sweat copper pipes with a blowtorch. My neighbor across the street, Dave, he nearly did that trying to fix a leak in his basement. The fire department had to come out and everything. They brought that big ladder truck, the one they usually keep at the station over by the highway. \n\nBut, uh, taking care of myself? I don't know. I guess I'm just getting by. I haven't really paid attention.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you don't care about the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been a while now. It's hard to put an exact date on it, but... I used to love messing with my tomato plants out back. Had these beautiful Cherokee Purples last year. And I'd spend hours out in the garage, just sanding wood at my bench, getting it perfectly smooth. I'd watch the Falcons games on Sundays, and stay after church talking with the folks for an hour or more. \n\nSpeaking of the garage, I need to get the tension spring on the main door looked at. It's starting to make this awful groaning noise when it rolls up. You gotta be careful with those torsion springs, though. My uncle Bob, he tried to fix his himself back in '94, I think it was. Slipped with the winding bar and nearly took his thumb clean off. Had to rush him to the ER in his old Buick LeSabre. That car was a boat, I tell you. Floated over potholes like they weren't even there. They don't make suspension like that anymore. Now you feel every pebble on the road. The city's supposed to be repaving our street next month, but they've been saying that since last fall. They brought out all those orange barrels and just left them sitting on the curb for weeks. The neighborhood kids started moving them around at night, making little obstacle courses for their bikes. \n\nBut... yeah, lately I just look at the garden, the workbench, the football games... and it's like it all belongs to somebody else. I just don't care about any of it anymore.\n\nDoctor: How long have you felt like you don't care about the things you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess... if I really think about it, this whole change in me, it's been going on for about two weeks now. Right around the time my daughter left Georgia for the military. \n\nGeorgia, you know, it gets so incredibly humid this time of year. You step outside and it's like walking into a wet blanket. I remember when we first moved down there, we had this old window AC unit in the bedroom. It rattled so loud you couldn't even hear the TV over it. I tried putting some cardboard under the edges to stop the vibrating, but it didn't do much. You really need those foam weather stripping strips for that kind of thing. You can get a roll of it at the hardware store for a couple bucks. The guy who runs the place down the street from me, Frank, he always knows exactly what aisle everything is in. He's been running that store for thirty years. Says his grandfather built the original building back in the twenties. They used actual old-growth pine for the framing back then, you know. Wood was just denser. You try driving a nail into a piece of that old pine and it'll bend right over if you don't drill a pilot hole first. \n\nBut yeah, it's been about two weeks since she left, and I just haven't cared about much of anything since.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling like you need to move around or do something physical to relieve your emotional state?\n\nPatient: No, not really... it's actually the opposite. I feel like my body is just moving through mud. People at work keep asking me if I'm sick because I'm walking so much slower than usual. It's taking me forever just to answer simple questions. \n\nSpeaking of work, we had this massive shipment of corrugated boxes come in last Tuesday. They were supposed to be the heavy-duty double-wall kind, you know, for shipping automotive parts. But the supplier messed up and sent single-wall. You try putting a 40-pound brake rotor in a single-wall box, the bottom just falls right out. I told the kid on the forklift, Tommy, I said, \"Don't even bother stacking those on the high racks.\" Tommy's a good kid, though. He's trying to pay his way through community college. He's got this old Honda Civic that he's always tinkering with in the parking lot during his lunch break. Put an aftermarket exhaust on it that sounds like a swarm of angry bees. I don't get why these young guys want their cars to be so loud. Back in my day, if your car sounded like that, it meant you blew a hole in your muffler and you needed to get to Midas before you got a ticket. \n\nBut yeah, I don't feel like moving around much at all. Just everything feels so slow and heavy.\n\nDoctor: Do you think these feelings of worthlessness and guilt are interfering with your daily life nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's every single day. I just... I can't get it out of my head. I keep going over my daughter leaving, just replaying it, telling myself I must have failed her somehow. Like, if I was a better father, I would have done something different and she wouldn't have felt the need to pack up and join the military. \n\nThe military, man... they sent her down to Texas for basic. San Antonio, I think. It gets hotter than a furnace down there. I drove through Texas back in the late eighties, hauling a load of lumber before I moved into management. Had this old Peterbilt cab-over. No AC, just the vent windows cranked open, blowing hot air in your face like a hair dryer. We used to stop at this diner right off I-10... I can't remember the name of it, but they had the best chicken fried steak. Plate as big as a hubcap, smothered in that white pepper gravy. The waitress there, she had this big hair, you know, like they wore back then, sprayed stiff as a board. She told me her husband was a mechanic for John Deere. Those old John Deere tractors, the green and yellow ones, they run forever if you keep the oil clean. You gotta check the filters, though. People forget about the fuel filters and then wonder why the engine starts sputtering when they're trying to bush hog a field. It's just basic maintenance, you know? You take care of the machine, it takes care of you. \n\nBut... what was I saying? Oh. Yeah. Just sitting at my desk at work, staring at the shipping manifests, and all I can think about is how I messed up with her. It just eats up my whole day."
  }
